If a DM works at building the aura of awe around such items as a master work suit of armor by a legendary armorsmith will be valued, even they don’t immediately convey tremendous defensive bonuses. Such items should be kept rare and expensive in order to increase their allure. After all, convincing a player that a suit of armor crafted by the incomparable Coralon is a worthy treasure is only going to be possible if the character in question isn’t constantly bumping into other people wearing similar items.

In addition to the possibilities presented by creating individual craftsmen of great repute, many games take as a given that a particular race or nationality has an innate ability for working metal. Whether it be dwarven armor or Damascene steel, there is a lot of room for DMs to create unique items of special significance.

## Variations on the theme

In reality, many complicated suits of armor have to be custom made to fit their owner. Consider making it necessary for adventurers to seek out a master craftsman to have their new-found magical plate armor altered. Make this particularly nerve- wracking by insisting that characters leave the armor with the smith for several weeks. Perhaps the master is particularly old, crusty, and absent-minded…

It can add color to a campaign to designate certain exotic types of armor as being only made/worn by members of a particular race, class, social caste, guild, or faith. Choosing armor types from a radically different historical period or culture can make this a particularly powerful plot device. A race of mysterious goblinoids wearing an unknown style of laminated wooden armor is more interesting than yet another goblin in leather togs.

Other possible reasons for visiting an armorer include maintenance and repair, commissioning of particularly extravagant items (solid gold breastplates etc), or appraising or selling loot acquired from the latest dungeon crawl. Especially creative players may want to design and have made a brand new style of armor, and will need to find a craftsman to transform their brilliant ideas into cold steel.

## Sample Armorer’s Shop: The Talon Turned

((Text to be read to players))

You have heard many tales of the wondrous craftsmanship of Coralon the Armorer. To stand now before his workshop, the famed “Talon Turned”, and weighed down by sufficient coin to afford his service, you cannot help but feel a stir of excitement. Heroes and kings go forth to battle shrouded in steel from the forge of Coralon, and soon you too will be unassailable in your new suit of mail…

((End of player text))

## Overview

Coralon the Armorer is known throughout the Kingdom and beyond as the finest craftsman working today. Certainly there are those who will claim that finer work is done deep in the Dwarven mountains or even in the shadows of the Elven woods, but none of those smiths have a shop right here in the city!

Coralon has become quite wealthy as a result of his work and his shop is located in one of the finest merchant districts in the city. As his fame spread, he decided to relocate from his home village here to this teeming metropolis, as only the wealthiest patrons can now afford his work. “What is your life worth?” he will ask any who balk at paying the going rate for his work.

The shop is large and spacious, with a small showroom out in front. Although the showroom has a multitude of items on display, these are intended as examples, to help clients decide what they want made. Coralon works almost entirely on direct commission. He crafts individual pieces for each client. The items in the showroom are in fact of excellent quality but he is reluctant to sell them as this will necessitate making replacements. He can be talked into parting with basic items – shields, helms, gauntlets – by anyone willing to pay vastly inflated prices, but convincing him to part with one of the display suits of armor would require an astronomically large sum of money. Coralon works exclusively in metal. He considers the creation of “lesser” armor to be beneath his talents.

Coralon’s shop is run by the master himself, and his much younger and prettier wife Deliah, who runs the showroom. He has three apprentices at various stages of development who help in the workshop.

Although his shop holds very valuable merchandise, Coralon employs no visible

security. Even at night, the only visible sign of protection is the closed and locked doors and shutters. This is possible because of the invisible protection Coralon enjoys – a close and personal relationship with both the legal authorities and the powers that rule the underworld. Between the two, these groups see that Coralon’s riches tempt no one. He considers the occasional gifts of some of his better work to be a small price to pay to cultivate these relations.

## Commissioning Armor

As noted above, Coralon does not sell “off the shelf”. Anyone seeking to engage the armorer to create a suit or piece of armor will need to come equipped with both patience and money. Coralon is constantly busy and there is generally a waiting list for his work. When accepting a commission he will warn the purchaser that it will be 1d6 months before he can even start on the project due to previous commitments. Coralon takes these commitments very seriously and rumor has it he has even made kings wait their turn, though few believe that to be true.

Even once the work begins, it will take at least twice as long for Coralon to craft the work as it would normally. This is because of the painstaking attention to detail that he pours into each piece. He will not work faster, considering any efforts to goad him into do so as an insult to his art. Those who pester him excessively have been known to grow old waiting for their piece to be finished.

Everything that Coralon produces should be considered masterwork. Customers should expect to pay a minimum of three times the usual price for any given masterwork item (that is, three times the usual masterwork price!). If the piece is to be particularly ornate or involves precious metals or stones, the price can be ten times or even more.

((Begin table))

## Class of Work	Cost Modifier	D20 Armor Bonus

Standard	3 X Masterwork	+1

Exceptional	6 X Masterwork	+2 or +3

Extravagant	10 X Masterwork	+2 or +3

((End Table))

## Shop Exterior

Coralon’s shop is housed in a sturdy, well-made building of timber construction. The building has a small loft above the shop where the apprentice’s sleep, and a basement where coal and raw materials are stored. Outside the rear of the shop there is a double door for deliveries of supplies a set of door into the ground covering a chute that allows coal to be shoveled directly into the basement.

The building is tastefully in burgundy with beige trim. A fancifully lettered sign showing an ornate shield hangs in front of the shop, proclaiming “Coralon’s”. No one who has need off, and can afford, his services would need any further clarification.

## Showroom Interior.

Entry to the shop is through a set of double doors in the front of the building. When the shop is open these are usually flung open to the street. Inside the double doors is the small showroom where examples of Coralon’s work are displayed. The showroom is relatively cramped, being an add-on to the original shop. It has a low ceiling (8’) and thin but sturdy wooden walls. Two windows in the front wall let light into the shop.

Along one of the side walls is a tall shelving unit holding examples of helmets, gauntlets, and other small armor pieces. These vary from the relatively simple to the quite complex and ornate, but anyone knowledgeable about armor who examines the pieces will quickly determine that they are all exceptionally well made.

Four lifelike mannequins stand around the floor of the showroom draped with various styles of armor. They are each unique and each displays a different type of armor and a different degree of utility versus style.

1. Mannequin 1: Suit of delicate and intricately decorated chain mail. This armor is quite light and would serve better as attire at a noble’s court than on the battlefield. Still, it is excellent armor and would turn most blades. Although Coralon does indulge clients who want to look like warriors more than be warriors, he does insist that all his armor be functional.

1. Mannequin 2: Suit of sturdy half plate armor including a full face helm. This armor is clearly made for a professional soldier. Its cut and styling is all practical and it is intended to spare the wearer from harm in the depths of the most violent battle.
1. Mannequin 3: Suit of surprisingly light but strong scale mail. This armor is made for those who may face danger but do not need the protection of a full suit of armor. Popular with adventurers who need more mobility than they can get with plate, the scale is among Coralon’s best sellers.

1. Mannequin 4: Deluxe suit of full plate armor with elaborate helm and gilded design etched into all major surfaces. The ultimate poser’s armor, this design would look very out of place in battle. It is, however, exquisitely crafted, so the wearer would be well protected if he were so foolish as to take this artwork into harm’s way.

1. On the opposite wall from the shelves of armor pieces is a small selection of shields hung up for display. Coralon can fashion virtually any style of shield in varying degrees of gaudiness. Coralon employs some of the finest artists in the city to decorate his shields with the appropriate heraldic symbols or other designs requested by the clients.

1. A short low counter stands at the back of the room where Deliah spends most of her time when there are no customers in the shop. The counter has several drawers in it where she keeps her measuring tools for use in taking a client’s exact measurements for Coralon. There is also a small moneybox here containing only small change – a total of

$100 in silver pennies. Coralon’s creations cost a great deal of money and payment is rarely made in cash on the spot.

The showroom is separated from the workshop by two heavy black curtains which hang from the ceiling.

## Workshop Interior

Once past the black curtain, one enters the workshop of Coralon the armorer. The room is well lit by large windows as well as the rear double doors which are generally open to allow cooler air into the room. The center of the floor is dominated by a large stone furnace and a large metal-hooded chimney which hangs above it.

1. The stone ringed furnace stands towards the center of the room. The stone outer ring stands some four feet high and contains the extreme heat of the constantly burning fire. Above the flames, a metal hood captures most of the smoke and funnels it upwards through a metal chimney to the exit in the ceiling.

1. Next to the fire stands a sturdy anvil and several barrels of water. The anvil has clearly seen a lot of use and is worn and battered. Coralon has had it almost his entire

career and would never part with it. He often jokes that it is the magic of the anvil that makes his work special, though this is not true in any literal sense.

1. A series of workbenches line one wall in the shop. This is clearly an area set aside for early work, including drawing and designing pieces, as well as the assemblage of intricate sections of armor. The tools are all carefully numbered and stored, each in their own correspondingly numbered slot or hanging from the appropriate hook. Coralon’s tools are themselves masterwork quality and he knows them all intimately. A simple glance will tell him if anything is out of place. At any given time there will be one or two works in progress on the benches, usually one commissioned work and one “experimental” project.

1. On the opposite wall from the main workbenches is a small work area where finishing work is done. This is where pieces are assembled and, in the case of the gaudier commissions, embellished with gilding or jeweled accessories. This bench is also very orderly and contains three deep drawers which are reinforced with sturdy locks for storing precious metals and stones.

1. These stairs lead down into a large basement storage area which contains raw materials (metal stock etc), coal, and sundry other supplies. Deliveries are made to an outside door set into the ground (much like an old root cellar) or through a coal chute set into the base of the outside wall. The cellar is larger than the actual footprint of the building and is sturdily built. Like everything else in the shop, Coralon insists that the storage area be kept tidy and organized – a task for whichever apprentice has annoyed him on any given day.

1. This ladder leads upstairs to a small attic loft shared by the three apprentices, Wilf, Jon, and Kyle. These youngsters are at varying points in their studies with one, Jon, almost ready to strike out on his own.

## City Garrison Barracks

**Role in the Campaign**

Within most organized settlements above a certain size, there exists a force of some kind dedicated to either keeping order, or maintaining oppression. These guardsmen or constables serve a necessary function, for in their absence the resulting anarchy would prevent the social order necessary for the establishment and flourishing of a city. Neither particularly lucrative nor glamorous, the City Watch attracts either those truly dedicated to service, those with skill at arms but down-on-their-luck, or those seeking an excuse to exert their will over others and indulge their sadistic tendencies.

Smaller villages often rely on either a part-time militia, or the local retainers of the manorial lord to perform law enforcement functions. Large town and especially cities, however, have evolved large, standing forces to keep the peace. In these urban centers, there are usually several barracks, spread throughout the city, where local guard units reside and train.

Guardsmen tend to live modestly but comfortably, as most city administrations recognize the wisdom of a relatively contented protection force. With power comes temptation, however, and almost all city garrisons have trouble with corruption. When gold flows freely in the marketplaces, why settle for silver? Guilds of all types like to keep at least a few guardsmen on the payroll just in case, and any self-respecting crime lord will have a few officers of the Watch in his pocket.

They tend to live in communal barracks, with perhaps a few private rooms for officers. Obviously this life lends itself more to those without families, but in some cultures it is normal even for married guardsmen to live at least part-time in garrison with their shield mates. The conditions in these garrison barracks will vary, but there is limited privacy and constant motion and noise.

Although the quality of the local City Watch will vary with location (and with the wealth of the community in question), in general guardsmen tend to be at the bottom end of the martial food chain, competent to corral drunks or deter petty thievery, but generally individually unable to stand up to an enraged barbarian or offended adventurer. Of course, the Watch isn’t expected to fight fair, and there are usually a lot of them, so adventuring parties would do well to remember that these women and men carry with them the full weight of the law of the city and of the realm. While defeating them in combat might not prove much of a challenge, escaping the long arm of retribution might…

## Variations on the theme

In some cities, garrisons may also serve as central civic administration buildings. These structures will be more elaborate, encompassing office space for the local bureaucracy, and will tend to be less austere. Citizens seeking services may have to convince the local guards of their need before being allowed inside. This arrangement is particularly convenient for the local administration, as it improves the security of their important papers, seals, records, etc.

In some campaigns, the law is kept by garrisons headed by nobles, knights, or Paladins dedicated to the upholding of law and justice. These garrisons will obviously be more effective, having better morale and being generally well led.

The quality of the law enforcement in a city often depends upon the quality of the laws. In some cities, the garrisons will be made up of fanatics, religious or otherwise, whose sole goal in life to make sure that everyone conforms to a very strict code of conduct. Unwary travelers entering a city with odd and vigorously enforced bylaws, may soon find themselves on the wrong end of an encounter with the Watch.

## Sample: City Barracks

((begin text to be read to players))

You stand before the imposing façade of the central barracks. Although only a low, one-story building, the guards stationed in front of the various doors lend to the structure an imposing air of menace. The dusty assembly area in front of the hall is empty now, though in a few hours it will be filled with grim, clanking guards lined up stoically for roll call before heading into the city for their shift.

_((end player text))_

## Overview

This garrison consists of four squads of twenty guardsmen each. The building is divided into four squad bays with two common areas and a central hall holding the sergeants’ quarters as well as the stairs down to a small armory. Meals are taken in a separate mess hall and privies are located behind the building in a large communal outhouse. A well is also located in the rear courtyard.

## Barracks Exterior

The barracks building is built of grim, grey stone and is a bleak and dark single story affair that is part dormitory and part police station. The only windows are small and narrow, located high up on the walls to allow a little air and even less light to enter. These windows are less than 6 inches wide and about eight inches long. From the exterior it is readily apparent that no adult human could squeeze through these openings.

In the hollow area in front of the main entrance and between the two wings is a parade ground where each squad musters prior to heading out on patrol. The square is cobbled with rough stone, and the thump of the guards’ hard soled boots on the stones echoes loudly as they go through the motions of their close order drill.

At the end of the square is the main entrance to the building. There are always two guards stationed here and they control access to the building through the thick double doors. Anyone with a credible story will be allowed in to see the sergeant-of-the-watch who will take their complaint. Unless the city is under a state of alert, the double doors will be open during the day as they are the main source of light and air for the dark stuffy building.

The opposite side of the building also has a pair of heavy iron-reinforced doors, but these are normally closed. A single guard stands here, directing any visitors to the main doors at the front. This guard post is generally used as punishment duty because it is inevitably dull and, because the building’s outdoor latrine is located in the courtyard behind the building, the stench is particularly foul.

## Barracks Interior

The inside of the barracks is similarly Spartan in design and decoration. Ceilings are eight feet high and the floors are well-worn hardwood planks. The basement armory is considerably more cramped, with barely a six foot ceiling and a packed earth floor.

Much of the barracks consists of personal living space. Should anyone choose to search through the occupants’ personal possessions, use the appropriate table in Annex A to determine what items of particular interest or value are found. Consult the table once per container or location (i.e. once for a chest and then a second time for a desk in the same room).

1. **Main Entrance.** Heavy double doors, reinforced with iron, stand open most of the time. Two sentries stand to either side of the doorway. The guards wear heavy leather armor and are armed with daggers, cudgels, and lethal-looking spears. Guards on door duty are generally disgruntled as this duty is viewed as one of the least desirable daily duties (though not as bad as guarding the back door).

The sentries will gruffly turn away any petitioners without an appropriately urgent need to enter. Anyone seeking to report a crime or conduct other legitimate-sounding business with the Watch will be permitted entrance. They are too bored to interrogate visitors much beyond the basic reason for their visit but suspicious behavior will cause them to perk up and ask further questions. In the event of a problem, they will summon the sergeant-of-the-watch whose desk is just inside the door.

1. **Sergeant-of-the-watch.** A sturdy desk and chair stand just inside the doors. The on-duty Sergeant is usually found here. The garrison Sergeants are encouraged to be gruff and unfriendly, thus discouraging any but the most desperate or sincere from filing a complaint. Elmore, the day watch Sergeant, tends to more genuinely sympathetic to the plight of victims of crime, but Amos the night watch Sergeant is a bitter man who prefers not to suffer fools.

Should a visitor have a legitimate complaint or concern the Sergeant will summon one of the stand-by patrols and send them to investigate. If the problem is urgent or life threatening, the Sergeant may accompany the patrol depending upon the perceived gravity of the problem. In the event of truly dire circumstances he will stand-up the off- duty squad and intervene in force.

More mundane complaints are recorded in a large ledger to be reviewed by the officer of the watch when the day and night watches turn over.

1. **Sergeant Amos’ Room.** Rank does have its privileges. While the rank and file guardsmen live in common barracks rooms, the two sergeants are entitled to at least a minimal amount of privacy. The tiny chamber barely has room for its furnishings, but it has a thick door that blocks out most noise from outside.

Sergeant Amos is in charge of the night watch and from the look of his room it is clear that he is not a neat individual. The room is strewn with possessions and the bedding looks suspiciously unwashed. Bits of clothing cover the bed and hang from the chair as well as several hooks behind the door.

A sturdy bed sits along one wall with a lumpy but serviceable mattress. A small desk covered with papers, simple personal items, and assorted clutter takes up most of the south wall. The chair has a definite wobble to it but has a plump hand-sewn cushion to add comfort to its hard seat.

Against another wall sits a large chest in which the sergeant keeps his personal items. In addition to anything rolled up on the random contents table, the chest contains a pouch with $500, a masterwork dagger, and a small notebook. The book appears to be a diary in which the Sergeant complains at great length about the many people in town who apparently conspire against him. A careful perusal of the text will lead to the inescapable conclusion that the sergeant is mentally unstable and possibly dangerous. His list of grievances varies from the wench who sold him watery beer to his superiors who deny him his rightful promotion.

1. **Sergeant Elmore’s Room.** The Day Watch Sergeant’s room is physically identical to his compatriot’s except that it is meticulously kept and clearly belongs to a military man. The walls are adorned with exotic weapons, each personally collected by Sergeant Elmore in his younger days as a wandering sell-sword. Rumor among the guards is that he took each of them off of the bodies of foes he had slain, but in actuality this is only true of perhaps half of the weapons. The others were purchased or bartered for.

All of the room’s furnishings are similar to those in Sergeant Amos’ room but are generally better maintained and in better repair. The large chest contains a variety of personal items including a small hand-written volume of surprisingly good quality poetry which the Sergeant will adamantly deny being the author of (though he is). There are two coin pouches containing $200 and $600 respectively, as well as a good quality pair of soft leather boots.

1. **Stairs to Armory.** These stairs lead down into the basement armory where the garrison stocks large quantities of common weapons and some basic supplies to withstand a short siege. Anyone searching the room can find basic provisions (iron rations, barrels of water etc) as well as spears, daggers, maces, cudgels, staves, short swords, and helms and shields.

1. **Squad Bays.**  The four squad bays are essentially identical. The contents of the various personal lockers can be determined on the random contents table in Appendix A (assumes the random item tables are completed!).

Each bay contains ten bunkbeds and twenty personal lockers. The beds are wood- framed and covered with thin mattresses and personal linens and blankets. The lockers are six feet tall and are divided vertically into two halves. The left half contains 4 evenly spaced shelves while the right half hold clothing hanging from a series of hooks.

Sergeant Elmore ensures that the guards maintain discipline by keeping their squad bays tidy at all times. The bays belonging to on-duty guards will be deserted, with the beds neatly made and the personal lockers securely closed (there are no locks on them, despite their name). Bays containing the off-duty squads will generally contain guards who are relaxing or sleeping. Due to Sergeant Elmore’s high standards, the guards spend considerable time maintaining their equipment as none want to draw the Sergeant’s wrath and end up on rear door duty.

In the common area between bays there is a large table around which the men sometimes gather to play cards, or simply gossip and gripe. Sergeant Amos, and his increasingly erratic behavior, are common sources of complaints, but in general the squads have high morale due to Sergeant Elmore’s professionalism.

1. **Rear Courtyard.** This is where the sentry stands controlling access to the rear door of the barracks. The air here is generally foul due to the latrine, and the duty is dull as virtually no one ever approaches the rear door (preferring the sweeter air of the front courtyard). During the day, the sentry will likely be bored but generally alert because Sergeant Elmore invariably makes several surprise inspections of the guard post.

At night however, given Sergeant Amos’ disinterest in his men, there is a 30% chance that this sentry will be dozing lightly. During the night shift, the doors are tightly

barred from within though, so simply getting past the guard won’t help an infiltrator all that much.

The courtyard contains the garrison’s well. Given the proximity to the outhouse, no one in their right mind drinks this water. It is generally used for washing and cleaning but will only be consumed in an emergency. Anyone drinking from the well has a 20% chance of getting a debilitating case of diarrhea and vomiting.

1. **Latrine.	This wooden structure holds a common latrine with six individual stations. Two large barrels of well water are kept filled for washing up.

## General Store

**Role in the Campaign**

Outside of cities, rural residents often depend upon their local shopkeepers to provide them with those necessary items they can’t make themselves and those rare luxury items they sometimes crave. In many villages or hamlets, one merchant is the source for many of these products. The general store serves as a central locale within the settlement; a lynchpin around which much of the daily traffic of the village takes place. More than just a place to buy goods, it is a center of social activity or of gossip mongering.

The merchant who runs the general store is generally one of the leading citizens in a small community and an essential ally in allowing small independent freehold farmers to survive the vagaries of the weather and crop yields. Many a local farmer owes his

survival to the willingness of the general store keeper to extend credit or forgive debt.

For outsiders, the general store is often the first, or even only, contact that travelers may have with a small community. Pilgrims, other merchants, adventuring parties, all have needs that can be met by the general store. Whether seeking supplies for a trip into the hills to prospect for gold or outfitting a party for delving into ancient dwarven tombs, at some point a trip to the general store becomes necessary.

Depending upon the sophistication of the transportation network in a given region, these merchants may be able to special order particular items that they do not regularly carry. In more civilized areas it may be a matter of days or perhaps weeks before the sought after item can be delivered. In more remote areas it may take months for a special order to be received, it if ever is.

Larger, more wealthy stores may have their own wagon(s) which regularly haul supplies in, others may rely on other merchants or even itinerant peddlers to replace their stock. Obviously, stores which depend upon the whims of peddlers are rarely in a position to accurate predict when or if they may receive a particular item. Fortunately, most general stores in rural areas are able to maintain basic stocks of items by dealing locally and supporting the local economy.

Although the general store will often be run by a true pillar of the community, no small number of them are owned by avaricious souls who are not adverse to making more profit off of strangers than locals. Some less scrupulous merchants may also offer goods of lesser quality to those unlikely ever to be back to complain. These store keepers tend to become good judges of character by necessity – it’s a poor idea to sell a faulty coil of rope to anyone who looks like they could survive a long fall and seek vengeance!

Extra rope? Iron rations? Yet another 10-foot pole? Head on over to the store lads, and stock up!

## Variations on the theme

Consider making the local general store is just that – local. They don’t much like outsiders around these parts, and characters had best not be thinking that their fancy ironmongery shirts and bulging money bags will impress these simple folks. Fact is,

adventurers mean trouble, and while the shopkeeper won’t likely refuse to sell to them, he certainly will charge premium prices. If they don’t like it, they can always shop somewhere else. Oh wait…

The general store in particularly remote villages may be sources of work for adventuring parties down on their luck and not too proud to take on the glamourless task of escorting wagon loads of provisions.

Maybe the general store holds monthly estate auctions of locals (and/or hapless adventurers) who have passed away recently. These events could include the local equivalent of community garage-sales, in which villagers spent a festive day browsing their neighbors tables, loaded down with assorted unwanted paraphernalia. The day could culminate in the estate auction, with some potentially valuable items going up for bid unrecognized by the unsophisticated country folks.

## Wilberforge’s General Store

((begin text to be read to players))

You’ve seen it before. In a dozen villages on the adventuring path, you’ve pulled your horse up before the fading painted façade of the local general store. The locals usually look at you suspiciously, from their perch on the front porch (not unreasonably, considering your conspicuous display of swords, axes, daggers, and other implements of destruction) but you know that the heft of your coin pouch will win you acceptance from the merchant within…

((end text to be read to players))

## Overview

The general store is a relatively large building by rural standards, usually centrally located in a settlement. Often towns spring up around these stores, originally established to serve the surrounding farms and homesteads.

Like many such stores, the one owned by Mabel Wilberforge is a moderately successful business and the central meeting point for her community. Mabel, and her late husband Hans, first established the store some forty years ago, when the region was

considerably less settled, and watched a thriving village grow up around them. Any outsiders asking questions about the area or its inhabitants are well advised to go directly to Mabel. Local lore has it that she knows everyone within twenty miles and can tell you what each of them had for breakfast. If her actual knowledge of local culinary habits falls a bit short of this boast, there is no question that she is a fountain of knowledge of value to the astute adventurer.

## Shop Exterior

The Wilberforge General Store is a two-story wood framed building built to last. The exterior shows the care with which the family has maintained their shop. The bright red and white paint on the walls and trim is only a couple of years old, and the front veranda remains proudly level without a hint of sag. The large front windows display a multitude of goods and the glass is clean and clear. The craftsmanship of the panes suggests that they were imported from outside the small community, as no one has the skill to produce large sheets of glass locally.

The first floor of the store is public space, used for the storage, display, and sale of goods. The second floor, with smaller windows covered with tasteful curtains, is the living space of the Wilberforge family.

The front of the store includes a large porch with a short staircase on each side. Several benches and chairs sit on the porch, usually occupied by locals, stopping by for their daily dose of news and gossip. Mabel herself is often found on the porch, holding for on any number of subjects upon which she considers herself an expert.

## Shop Interior

Customers enter the store via two large double doors off of the porch. Immediately in front of them is a long counter where Mabel usually sits (when not jawing on the porch). From her perch on a stool behind the counter, she greets regular customers warmly and strangers diffidently. Over the years she has seen every shade of character come through the door and she is an excellent judge of character. She is a pious woman who is appropriately deferential to ecclesiastic characters such as good-aligned clerics. She will watch “undesirable” types closely to try to prevent thefts.

Once greeted by Mabel, visitors are free to browse the shop. Tall shelves line all of the outside walls of the main room, crammed full of sundry items of use to rural folk and travelers. Another long counter stands between customers and these shelves,

running the full length of each wall, interrupted periodically by small vertical hatches which raise to allow Mabel or other members of her family to move out from behind the counter.

The store is roughly divided into five sections, although many goods could arguably be found in any of the sections. The placement of goods is somewhat haphazard, depending more upon available shelf space than a strict application of the divisons into sections. The sections are Household Goods, Outdoors/Travelling Suppplies, Farming Tools and Hardware, Food Stuffs, and Luxury Items.

The shop tends to stock most of the basic items one would expect within these sections. In addition, specialty items can be ordered but this can take some time and will prove costly. It is often more cost effective for those seeking scarce items to simply travel to the city to get them themselves. Enquiring about rare or magical items will elicit either incredulity or teasing from Mabel (who may offer the questioner her “magic” chamber pot which empties itself – much to the amusement of onlookers on the porch).

In a break in the shelves along one wall is a small, sturdy door which leads into a back store room. This room is choked with boxes, crates, barrels, bags, and other containers full of products. The room is chaotic with little or no organizations. The store room used to be Hans’ job, along with ordering and book keeping, and since his passing last year, the store has suffered from a lack of organization behind the scenes. Mabel has always prided herself on running the front of the store well, but has little interest in the behind-the-scenes aspects of the business.

Customers usually find several members of Mabel’s extended family behind the counters or stocking the shelves. Mabel and Hans had three sons and four daughters, and these have produced a dozen grandchildren (so far). Various cousins, uncles, aunts, and assorted nieces and nephews also live in the area. One jokster remarked that it was impossible to fire a crossbow within five miles of the store without drawing at least some Wilberforge blood. (The thrashing applied by several of the Wilberforge cousins ensured that this joke was rarely repeated). One son and all of the daughters are still living.

Wilberforge kin most likely to be working in the store include: Ilse Wilberforge (the youngest daughter and something of a local spinster); Kevin Wilberforge (the eldest son and presumed heir-in-waiting); Kendra Kellick (Kevin’s daughter) and her husband Dorick. Other extended family members are present at unpredictable times.

Main Counter

The main counter is always manned, either by Mabel or one of the other family members. All customers are greeted here and eventually return to pay for their purchases. Under the counter Mabel keeps a knotty shillelagh as well as a cash box containing several hundred $ in various denominations.

Stairs

Behind the main counter a large staircase leads up to the family’s living quarters on the second floor. The bottom of the staircase is cordoned off with a knotted rope to prevent customers from wandering upstairs.

Household Goods Section

This section of the store contains a wide variety of items needed to maintain a household. Most of the goods are relatively inexpensive but well made. Mabel takes pride in maintaining a very well stocked household goods section since this makes up a large part of her business with the locals.

Gaol

**Role in the Campaign**

It goes without saying that in regions beset by monsters and maniacal mages, gaols and prisons are rarely places of much comfort. Indeed, they generally serve as places to shelve inconvenient persons or those whose misdeeds are not quite serious enough to warrant execution in one of its many gruesome forms. While many nobles maintain their own dungeons (as do some temples and other organizations eager to keep their own malefactors under wraps), local chartered municipal authorities are often responsible for the penal system within the town limits.

Generally small and unpleasant places, the gaols in such towns are rarely designed to hold criminals for long periods. Often they serve to restrain dangerous or deranged

individuals until they can be dealt with more permanently, or to hold suspected lawbreakers while investigations are made. Sometimes these investigations require the use of the implements of torture commonly found in such gaols. Depending upon the moral compass of the authorities, the local dungeon and torture chamber may be horrific or simply dreadful.

Unfortunately for adventurers and other roving strangers, gaols sometimes serve another purpose – that of income generation. Some small-town governments make a decent amount of money from fines extracted from outsiders who fall afoul of any of a multitude of local bylaws. Naturally, the authorities will generally avoid antagonizing any individuals or groups capable of violently resisting such extortion.

The treatment of inmates will depend very much upon the prevailing moral authority in charge of the gaol. It is important to keep in mind that in many societies, the concept of “innocent until proven guilty” is replaced by “if you weren’t guilty, you wouldn’t be in gaol”. Characters seeking to exercise what they think to be their “rights” may be in for a shock, as our more modern expectations of the rights of prisoners are unlikely to be met.

Regardless of the relative harshness of the legal system, it is safe to assume that even the more humane facilities will be dank, dark, unpleasant places in which no one should seek to tarry. Gaols tend to be infested with vermin (both guards and inmates) as well as bugs, rodents, and other creepy crawlies.

**Variations on the Theme**

- Upon arrival in a new city, a member of the party may fall afoul of an obscure local regulation and find himself escorted to the gaol. Contrary to expectations, the gaol is clean, neat, and even the cells are comfortably appointed with beds stuffed with relatively fresh straw. The food is nutritious, if plain, and the guards are polite and not at all abusive. After a couple of days of such treatment, prisoners may be very confused. This confusion will abate when they are politely invited in front of the local magistrate who explains that, unfortunately, local custom demands that anyone accused of any crime, no matter how minor, who cannot prove their innocence within three days is to be executed without delay. While it is unfortunate that the law is so harsh, it has proven effective in reducing recidivism.
- Perhaps the local gaol has been secretly infiltrated by a local evil cult that uses prisoners in its horrible rituals. Having a cultist in charge of the gaol makes it easier to acquire appropriate specimens.
- The dungeons below the gaol are rumored to link up with a long-forgotten tunnel complex built by an ancient dwarven kingdom. Folklore speaks of fortunes in gold and gems, abandoned by the dwarves when they fled centuries ago. The only way in is through the gaol.

**A “Gaol”** is a small local jail or similar type of building for incarceration of malefactors and criminals prior to their trial and sentencing. In some cases, imprisonment itself may be the sentence but this is rare since smaller localities cannot afford to upkeep a prisoner who provides nothing back to the community. In these cases, the prisoner may be used as free labor on various projects around the village or loaned out to local notables for dirty or dangerous work.

The Gaol will be a building that will have certainly seen its share of adventurers; be they imprisoned for disorderly conduct at the local Tavern, PC Thieves who are caught in the act of plying their trade upon locals or sometimes even simply because the local Shire Reeve (or “Sheriff”) didn’t like the looks of well-armed strangers in the community. If the campaign is set in a feudal setting, criminals will be brought before the local noble whose fief or landholding the village is located in. These “Hundreds Courts” have no jury, and the noble is judge, jury, and (at least the commander of) executioner. As nobles gain their wealth from the production of their local villagers, they will tend to take the side of locals in all but the most egregious cases. If the hamlet is more autonomous, the local Mayor will preside over a court, and while there will be a jury (9-12 persons) they will all be locals and will sentence crimes accordingly.

In most cases, paying of suitable fines and agreeing to vacate the environs of the hamlet are sufficient to get adventurers free of the local Gaol, but if a village has need of adventurers to solve a problem a bargain might be struck to waive fees/fines/time served in order to gain the mercenary services of martial and magical types. In the event that PCs believe that they can simply agree to a deal and then leave, the local Reeve might keep their coinage or mounts as collateral to insure their completion of tasks and subsequent return.

**Variations on a Theme:**

- After relaxing at a small tavern with a pint of ale to wash away the road dust, one of the party members (usually the least martial looking) is arrested by the local Reeve on a patently false charge. Upon further questioning, the Reeve informs the adventurers that his only daughter has been abducted and carried away to another village by the son of that village’s tavern keeper. As the village home of the abductor is in the fief of another lord, he has no authority to search for her there, and while his Lord/Mayor is attempting to intervene the Reeve is

concerned about his daughter’s safety. If the adventurers will go and return his daughter to him, the Reeve will then release the imprisoned party member and they can all leave with the thanks of the Reeve with them.

- 

This can be played as what it appears, or the daughter could have eloped and has no

intention of returning. The Reeve might have a feud with the other village’s Tavern keeper and is just looking to make trouble while his hands are clean.

- One or more of the adventurers are arrested for breaking an obscure local bylaw and are given only a day’s sentence to be served in the Gaol. The Gaol, unlike most of its kind, is clean and the prisoners are well treated. This could make party members suspicious, and the GM can have as much fun with this as they wish. Maybe the Reeve is a pious person and believes in treating people fairly? Maybe the Reeve has mistaken one of the PCs for a local noble who’s notorious for traveling in disguise and so he doesn’t want to chance being cruel to an aristocrat. Or maybe late in the night the PCs will find themselves bustled off into a slave caravan traveling through the village late at night, with the Reeve pocketing the commission (and the PC’s gear) while telling the villagers that the party left early. There are lots of angles that can be played on with the simple Village Gaol!

## Rural Inn

**Role in the Campaign**

At the end of a long day on the road, travelers of all types look forward to an evening at the inn. Whether they are well-appointed merchants, nobles riding fine horses, simple pilgrims trekking onwards in a show of devotion, or stalwart adventurers taking a well-deserved break from battling the forces of evil, the inn welcomes them all.

Few locales have the same cachet as a fantasy role playing inn. Many (dare I say most) early gamers started their careers by being plunked down at a table in a small, smoky inn, getting a drink from the retired adventurer-turned-innkeeper, and meeting up with a like-minded band of dungeon delvers. Often the inn and the local temple were the only non-dungeon environments ever encountered by those wide-eyed players.

Of course, in a more developed campaign, the inn plays a wider role than just a

place to recuperate hit points. These establishments are a mainstay of the caravan systems that tend to extend across most civilized game worlds. Transporters of goods and people often maintain contractual relationships with good quality inns across the country, allowing their employees and passengers to be assured of at least a minimal degree of comfort along the way. Very large corporations may actually open their own way stations at which travelers can stop, change horses, and rest in security.

Roadside inns dot the various major travel routes of the world, some luxurious and some more spartan. The best of them offer fine food, soft beds, and occasionally, superior entertainment. At worst they offer a relatively dry spot on a hard floor with a watery stew for dinner. Either way, the inn fills an important niche for all that travel.

Inns and hostels are not confined to major thoroughfares of course. Small establishments offer comfort to travelers throughout the civilized lands, and some even spring up on the frontiers of the wilds. Innkeepers are often consummate entrepreneurs and where a need exists for a safe place to rest up, odds are an inn will fill it.

These more remote inns tend to be quasi-fortified, often employing a staff of stalwarts to discourage any but the most determined of raiding parties. In territories where there are active bands of hostiles, humanoid or otherwise, innkeepers may seek informal truces or alliances to ensure the safety of their guests.

More than just a venue for shelter and food, the inn also offers an excellent venue for gathering information, networking, and other forms of social interaction. Prospective patrons abound for the industrious adventurer in search of a new sponsor and illicit deals are waiting to be made by the shadowy figure in the booth in the back. A variety of services tend to be available at rural inns. Some will also function as trading posts, or as informal messenger services. Other artisans occasionally set up around a particularly busy inn, forming an embryonic community that might, with time, develop into a full- fledged village.

## Variations on the Theme

A remote inn might serve as a cover for a local family of murderous intent. While most travelers will pass through the inn’s doors safely and without ever guessing how close they came to a brush with death, the occasional stranger, without close kin or friends nearby, will disappear and never be heard from again. These disappearances might be mundane cases of theft and murder, or they might be something more – a cover for a dark pact with evil forces of the underworld perhaps?

The establishment and protection of an inn in a remote, dangerous region would serve as a worthy undertaking for an adventuring party. Perhaps the local guild of Carters approaches the party with a request to escort the innkeeper’s family, oversee the construction and defense of a new inn in an area where the previous establishment was burned out by monsters/raiders/unknown forces.

Different races conceive of hospitality differently and each will have its own variation on the local inn. Some, like dwarves, will likely have large taverns with sleeping accommodations available, while comfort-loving halflings will have elaborate, high-class inns with excellent kitchens.

## Sample: The Hog’s Wattle Inn

((text to be read to players))

At the end of a long day, you are relieved to pull up in front of the Hog’s Wattle Inn, a respectable looking place (relatively speaking) that beckons to the weary traveler. The well-maintained building suggests a pride of ownership that will hopefully carry over to the dinner that your stomach has been loudly calling for these last few miles.

((end player text))

## Overview

The Hog’s Wattle – known affectionately simply as the Hog – is a moderately comfortable inn situated in a rural region along a major trade route. The inn caters to a wide variety of individuals and tastes as a result is larger than many roadside inns. A large common area and dining hall may suffice for the average traveler, but for those of more refined tastes (and larger wallets) private chambers and a couple of private dining rooms are available. An outside stable, staffed by an able hostler, also provides care and comfort for travelers mounts.

Roscoe “Jolly” Buridge, a former adventurer and notorious prankster, runs the inn. Jolly, so nicknamed for his merciless sense of humor, remembers well his days on the adventuring circuit and like most halflings, the things he missed most while on the road were soft sheets and good food. Now he tries to deliver these to others.

As an adventurer, Jolly stayed at many an inn, and he has used that experience to incorporate many of the best features of the places he enjoyed most. In particular he has ensured that his kitchen is well stocked and well staffed and that the Hog’s Wattle always provides top notch entertainment. Minstrels often include the Hog in their tours

as Jolly’s reputation as a fair employer with a fine wine cellar is well known.

The Hog is also a well-established gathering place for adventuring parties seeking new opportunities. Jolly has a special place in his heart for adventurers and is always ready to listen to even the most exaggerated tales of dungeon delving. In truth, he misses the life and tries to live vicariously through the exploits of others.

## Inn Exterior

The Hog is a three story, sturdy structure of wooden beam construction. Although every effort has been made to make the building look both inviting and aesthetically pleasing, a close examination will reveal that the inn is also easily defendable. Heavy wooden shutters complete with arrow slits can be quickly swung shut in front of the inn’s many windows. Being out in the wilds, the inn has on occasion had to fend off attacks by various raiders.

Around the outside of two sides of the building runs a sturdy, five-foot wide porch of cedar upon which sit numerous chairs and stools, perfect for having a quiet smoke on an autumn evening. Three staircases lead off the porch, a wide main set of stairs and two smaller secondary exits.

Off to one side of the main building is a similarly built and maintained barn which functions as a stable for guests. Like the main building it is built to be defensible in the event of a raid.

## Inn Interior Ground Floor

The inside of the inn is decorated as a testament to its owner’s career as an adventurer. The walls are adorned with trophies, weapons, paintings (most depicting various heroic scenes from popular stories and songs) and assorted dungeon delving accoutrements. Truth be told, most of these keepsakes were in fact purchased by the owner, a fact that enterprising adventuring parties might file away for a day when they need a little extra cash.

The central fire pit keeps the building invariably smoky, as the central chimney does a poor job of drawing as it is in need of a good cleaning.

1. Entry way.

Large heavy double doors open outwards onto the porch, leading to a small entrance hall where the innkeeper normally greets arriving guests. A small bell rings as the doors are opened, alerting the staff to the arrival. From the entryway guests can either proceed up the stairs to the private rooms, enter the inn’s common room, or pass into the proprietor’s personal area (not recommended without prior invitation).

The entryway holds a tall round table, upon which a guest book is always available. Most guests decline to sign their real names, and the book is replete with amusing puns and joke names.

1. Innkeeper’s room

Jolly maintains modest quarters here by the front door. From his room he can easily greet guests or supervise the activities in the kitchen, something he does often. His room contains an antique desk and chair which rumor has it were retrieved from an evil wizard’s tower. The mundane truth is that Jolly inherited it from his aunt Florence, but he is content to let the more interesting version of events go uncorrected. The desk has three sturdy drawers on the right side in which he keeps all his account ledgers as well as a relatively small amount of cash. ((insert cash amount)) His more substantial funds are well hidden in a secret area in the barn (see #8). These drawers are locked with good quality locks (DC 25).

His room also contains his comfortable bed, a set of shelves containing books and personal knickknacks, and a large trunk which contains his clothing and other personal items.

1. Kitchen

The inn’s kitchen is renowned throughout the surrounding lands. As befits an inn run by a halfling, the food is inevitably of exceptional quality and it draws diners from miles around. Much of the success of the inn lies in its ability to draw regular customers from the surrounding region, rather than in the itinerant travelers who seek shelter within. A common menu might look something like this:

Squab with garlic and fresh herbs Beef marrow fritters

Pie of the day (usually chicken or venison) Black bread

Take-yer-chance Stew (a collection of leftovers) Roast joint (usually pork)

((expand menu – add prices))

The genius is a young human cook named Guillaume Belair. After a life spent following his diplomat father from city to city, Guillaume entered an apprenticeship in the Elven court, where he mastered the art of preparing fine feasts. His wanderlust overcame him some five years ago and he set out to seek his fortune, stopping at the Hog when his money ran out. He enjoys working for Jolly, who gives him total freedom in the kitchen, and has built a solid reputation for the inn. It will be a sad day for the local community should Guillaume decide to move on, but for now he is content with his lot.

The kitchen is exceptionally well equipped, with two large ovens and two fire pits for roasting. Two scullions assist in the food preparation, doing everything from prepping vegetables to hauling water from the well outside. A long central table serves as a workspace, while hooks and slots along the walls hold a variety of utensils and cookware.

The kitchen opens into the common room, with another door leading into Jolly’s private quarters.

1. Stairs

This large, wide staircase leads up to the second floor where several private rooms and the staff quarters are found.

1. Common Room

This large room contains sixteen cots which can be rented for the night. The room offers no privacy, guaranteed noise and disturbances, and an affordable rate. Most travelers, unless they are wealthy or in need of private space, will opt for the common room. It is also the accommodation of choice for those who enjoy too much of Jolly’s ale and wake to find themselves owing money for a bed they had never intended to rent.

The cots are made of wood with some fresh straw tossed onto them from time to time. Jolly ensures that insect infestations are kept to a minimum and the room is kept surprisingly clean by the house maids. Jolly prides himself on providing security for guests and the common room is relatively safe. At the same time, no one in their right mind would leave valuables there unattended, but it has been several months since anyone was murdered in the room.

1. Dining Area

Two long tables provide dining room for more than twenty guests. While the inn

rarely has that many people renting rooms, the dinner table is often filled with locals out for some of Guillaume’s cooking. The tables are stained, scarred, and more than a little wobbly, but the quality of the food more than makes up for it.

It is considered a killing offence to interrupt a man’s supper at the Hog. People come a long way and pay a lot of money for their food – it is unwise to aggravate such folk.

1. Central Hall

The central hall has a large firepit in the middle, with a relatively ineffective chimney above it. Surrounding the it are a variety of chairs, benches, and stools, where most guests gravitate after dinner. This area is often the scene of impromptu performances by traveling minstrels or bards.

Particularly popular are the tall tales told by Old Jake, a grizzled and bedraggled looking dwarf of uncertain age. What his real name is, none seem to know, but Jolly calls him “Old Jake” and the name has stuck. Jake never seems to have any money, but his ability to tell vivid stories of adventure and far off lands has earned him a faithful following, so Jolly considers it a good investment to provide the old fellow with free food and wine to keep the guests entertained.

1. Stables

The inn maintains its own stables, room for up to ten horses indoors. Most travelers choose to leave their animals in the outdoor corral, but those concerned about losing their mounts to thieves or predators will pay extra for the security of the stable. The Ostler, Karl, and his two sons, Jurgen and William, maintain the stables and care for the animals left in their care.

## Upper Floor

1. Landing

Upon arrival at the top of the long staircase, guests are greeted by a husky man known only as Draj. The son of a local farming family, Draj is extremely strong but suffered a terrible head injury as a child, rendering him a little slow. He retains a pleasant, happy personality, even while escorting troublemakers out of the inn, but his speech is slow and childlike. He provides security for guests paying for the privilege of a private room, and the mere sight of him and his hefty club deters most crimes.

Draj is usually perched on a stool at the top of the stairs when not making periodic

rounds of the grounds.

1. Sitting Area

The upstairs guests have access to a private sitting area. Food and drink can be served here for paying guests and anyone they invite upstairs. One of the barmaid visits regularly to take orders and clear dishes.

The room is finely appointed with antique furnishings and tasteful art. None of the adventurer memorabilia of the common room can be seen here. Unlike the “fake” adventuring gear seen downstairs, most of the art work and fine glassware used here in the sitting are actually was acquired by Jolly during his adventuring days. He is very proud of this area and visits the guests often.

A large, double flued, brick chimney rises through the room from the central fire pit in the hall below dominates the room. A large fireplace faces the sitting area, providing heat during the cold season.

The chimney has become something of a local institution, serving as a kind of message board for wealthier travelers and patrons. Anyone, not just guests, may approach Jolly downstairs and ask to leave a message, but being as the chimney is in the private part of the inn, it is obviously only useful to leave a message for those prepared to pay for a room. It is usually festooned with at least a dozen notes stuck onto pins that protrude from the brickwork for that purpose. A stack of paper is always available beside the chimney, along with writing materials. Most of those using the service are regulars, trying to get in touch with other regular visitors.

1. Balcony

This upper floor balcony is available to guests. It holds a variety of comfortable chairs, and is a popular location from which to be seen by folks on the street below. “Taking a pint on the balcony” is understood as a local metaphor for relaxing or idling.

1. Staff Quarters

Jolly is a relatively enlightened employers, and has set aside a small but comfortable room for his six employees. Each as a small bed and a locked chest in which personal belongings are kept. These accommodations are considerably better than at many such establishments and are part of the reason that the Hog has such loyal employees. There is little of value in the room, but it is home to its occupants and they will react very badly to any intrusion into their private space.

1. Private Room A

This room is currently unoccupied. All five private rooms are furnished identically, but a letter distinguishes each. Amongst themselves, inn employees refer to guests by room letter, as in, “Hey Jolly, B wants clean linens!”

The private rooms contain two large comfortable beds with real mattresses, a large chest of drawers, and a couple of chairs. Other amenities can be provided at request. The rooms are lit by oil lanterns which can be adjusted to provide more or less light as required.

1. Private Room B

## Magic Guild

**Role in the Campaign**

In worlds where magic is commonplace, or at least not mythical, it is only natural that practitioners of the arcane arts should seek to come together for mutual benefit. In most fantasy worlds, such associations come into being in the form of guilds of wizards. Just as carpenters or innkeepers recognize the value in banding together, so too does it make sense for the magical community to pool its resources to better the welfare of its members.

Magic Guilds vary considerably from place to place and time to time. In some cases, where mages are generally accepted and respected members of society, the guilds serve simply as social clubs. Gatherings are held regularly to allow members a private, secure location to relax, share experiences, and compare notes. These guilds often contain libraries of rare books and scrolls and provide simple services such as research to members for a nominal fee. One city might have several such guilds, similar to the clubs that nobles set up as exclusive gathering places.

In other situations, guilds of mages can be grim, controlling organizations. Some guilds license the use of magic, striking against any non-members who dare to delve into the arcane arts without official sanction. These guilds generally try and regulate the teaching and use of magic, either because they jealously guard these secrets to increase their own power, or because they legitimately fear the havoc that could be wreaked by unschooled and undisciplined delving into forbidden arts.

In either case, the local Magic Guild is often the best place to seek advice, training, or assistance in all matter magical. Whether the need is for the identification of an obscure artifact pulled from an ancient tomb, or for the deciphering of a particularly vexing scroll, or the casting of particular magics, one would be well advised to seek out the guild for help. At the very least, the guild should be able to direct the adventurer to an appropriate practitioner of the arts.

Within the broader community, the Guild is often viewed with awe and not a small amount of suspicion. Even in settings where magic is commonplace, it will be viewed with trepidation by the simple folk who cannot use it. If the Guild is an active and generally supportive part of the community, it may well be accepted in the same way that other guilds are, but it will always retain a slightly different status, as a gathering of folk who perform strange and abnormal feats.

## Variations on the Theme

In a city where magic is tightly controlled by a tyrannical Magic Guild, a conflict might well arise through the attempted establishment of an alternate guild, one dedicated to the benevolent spread of magical knowledge to the common people. As in any power struggle, the force of the established guild will be turned against the upstarts, with potentially explosive consequences for the community. Adventurers might well be pulled into the conflict, either as mages or as agents of mages.

In a society ruled by mages, the Magic Guild might well serve the same function as a parliament. Membership in the ruling class is defined by the ability to cast spells, or to use magic of a particular type or difficulty level. Perhaps there is a statutory limit on the number of mages who can participate in the guild (and by extension in the government) and therefore a wizard seeking to enter the guild must challenge and defeat an incumbent in a magical duel in order to take his seat.

Practitioners of magic are notoriously eccentric. The local guild might well be an odd magical structure, changing shape or location on a regular basis. It might have no doors or windows, being accessible only via magical means. Restricting membership to

those able to cast the necessary teleportation magics to get inside might well be a policy of such a guild.

FILENAME \p C:\Documents and Settings\John\My Documents\Roleplaying Games\Backup GFW Essential Places Jan 17\Role in Campaign v10\Magic Guild v1.0.doc

## Noble Manor House

**Role in the Campaign**

A staple of many fantasy campaigns is the local Baron or manorial Lord who provides leadership for a rural area. Whether the setting is strictly feudal, with convoluted patterns of subinfeudation and fealty, or whether the local political and social system is more loosely defined, the local Lord is often a patron, ally, or foeman of the stalwart adventuring party.

Generally holding sway over a relatively small region populated by peasant farmers and perhaps a small village and local mill, the Lord is generally at the bottom of the nobility food chain. Manorial Lords usually owe allegiance to a more powerful noble and enjoy a variety of legal and customary powers and privileges. Though vastly better off than the peasants who farm their land, these nobles rarely live lives of ease and luxury.

Depending upon where the manor is within the realm, the manor house may be a simple fortified stone keep or an elaborate country house with extensive gardens and expensive fixtures. Obviously manor in less secure regions will require more martial trappings, often being surrounded by walls or at least a wooden or dirt palisade. Motte and bailey keeps are often used in frontier regions as manorial seats as they provide simple yet effect protection for the Lord and his forces.

In more civilized settings, the manor house has evolved into more of a mansion. Often surrounded by meticulously planned an maintained grounds, with a variety of

gardens, fountains, and statuary, these homes are clear displays of the wealth and privilege of their owners. They tend to have large staffs of servants, guards, and assorted hangers-on, and often function as small communities. The staff is often quite dedicated and status conscious, with a well-treated staff being a source of great support to the Lord, as they recognize their privileged role in serving him.

Local subjects of the manorial ruler are sometimes invited to annual feasts or festivals held on the grounds of the manor house. Often the harvest is celebrates, or else a particularly important religious holiday is observed with a day of games and merriment sponsored by the Lord.

Of course, not all manorial Lords are benevolent. Some are harsh masters, keeping their people in servitude and misery. The manor houses of such masters are naturally less jolly places, with fewer gardens and more gibbets on the grounds. In such cases, the manor house will have more guards and better defences, just in case the rabble decides to rise up.

## Variations on the Theme

Local manors are often good sources of employment for adventurers, as bandits, monsters, or other threats to their subjects often vex the local Lord. Some manors might have permanent housing for adventurers, kept on as retainers by the local sovereign. Such stable employment might chafe at adventurers blessed with a wandering spirit, but it can provide a base from which to conduct operations.

Some manor houses have been handed down through families for centuries. Often an extensive network of underground crypts has been constructed for the masters and mistresses of the manor. Such complexes provide plenty of adventure opportunity for stalwart heroes willing to figure out why all the coffins are suddenly empty…

One time-honored rite of passage for all adventuring parties is the establishment of their own stronghold and domain. Perhaps having rendered some service to their liege lord, the adventurers will be granted permission to claim a piece of wilderness as their own and build their own keep. Alternately, they might be granted title to an existing manor, and have to take on the existing house and lands with their existing staff and subjects.

## Sample: Cheltingdon Manor

((begin text to be read to players))

Atop a small hill, looking out over the crops of the tenant farmers, the manor house stands as a solid testament to the authority and majesty of the local nobility. Unlike the humble dwellings below, the house is built of sturdy stone and the outbuildings bustle with activity.

((end player text))

## Overview

This manor house belongs to Sir Percival Drakeson, only recently raised to hold this fief after long years service to the crown. Formerly a key member of the King’s privy council, Drakeson was named Lord of Cheltingdon Manor six months ago due to failing health. Bad lungs caused by an arrow wound many years ago, and worsened by the damp air of the royal castle, have left Drakeson without much hope of long life. He sought a hereditary manorial grant to ensure that his wife and three children would be comfortable after his impending demise.

## Manor Exterior

Cheltingdon Manor is a typical unfortified manor house. Its location deep inside the settled boundaries of the Kingdom, far from raiders or border strife, allows its holder to live without some of the more martial trappings of manors on the frontier. The house is built in a typical style, sitting up high upon the top of a small knoll.

As is common in such buildings, the ground floor is a windowless cellar with a long, narrow, exterior staircase leading up the outside of the manor house to the main floor, some ten feet above the ground. This provides security to the occupants by putting the door and windows out of easy reach of anyone seeking an illicit entry into the manor.

The two floors above the cellar have numerous windows, though those on the main floor are barred on the outside to further discourage unwanted guests. Only the windows on the top floor are without grills. The exterior walls are thick, sturdy stone from the local quarry.

## Manor Interior – Main Floor

1. Entry Stairs

The stairs outside the manor house rise steeply to a landing outside two heavy, ironclad wooden doors. The doors are generally barred from the inside and one of the Lord’s men-at-arms stands guard before them. Should visitors seek entrance, the guard will ring a bell, summoning the Lord’s Seneschal to speak with the petitioners.

1. Entry Hall

The grand entry hall is designed to awe visitors to Cheltingdon Manor. Like the other rooms on this floor, the room has a twelve-foot ceiling, and is dominated by an enormous crystal chandelier, holding over fifty lit candles.

The four corners of the room hold impressive statues, each depicting a former holder of the fief in full armor and regalia. These statues stand tall upon pedestals of marble, and have been sculpted by the finest artists of each Lord’s time. The current Lord Cheltingdon is having his own statue done, but has not yet decided which of his illustrious predecessors he will deplace.

The walls of the room are adorned with tapestries, each depicting an memorable event in the career of the current Lord. These are remarkably accurate, largely due to the truly heroic deeds actually done by Sir Drakeson, avoiding the need for much exaggeration. A series of benchs line one wall, for those waiting to be admitted to the Great Hall.

The center of the room holds a wooden staircase which leads up to the gallery on the second floor.

1. Staff Quarters

This room serves as the common living space for the household staff. Unlike many of his peers, Lord Cheltingdon has set aside an actual room to allow his staff their own quarters. Any guests (excluding distinguished visitors) are invited to bed down in the great hall, or with the soldiers in the barracks outside, depending upon their preference.

The room is modestly furnished, with each of the five household staffers having a bed and a chest in which to keep their meager possessions.

1. Chapel

As is the common practice, the Lord has established a private chapel on the main floor. It is dedicated to his patron deity, though care has been taken to make sure that proper homage is paid to the entire pantheon. The room is brightly lit during the day

by two large windows at the end which allow light to stream into the room. The room positively sparkles with reflections from the many gilded artifacts on display.

Lord Cheltingdon is a pious man, and unlike many of his peers, he actually pays more than cursory attention to his religious duties. His personal chaplain serves the religious needs of the entire household, and even provides instruction in reading and writing to children from the village.

The chapel is dominated by a large alter below the windows, depicting scenes from the holy texts of the pantheon, upon which sits a graven image of the Lord’s patron god. Twin statues of two of the god’s aspects stand to each side of the altar. A font of holy water, regularly renewed by the chaplain, stands to one side.

Three rows of pews provide seating for the faithful, or for the pupils when school is in session. The back of the chapel features a series of books cases containing a variety of religious and mundane texts. A careful search through the shelves has a 5% chance of turning up a rare text. Between the shelves sits a large statue of the god of knowledge, seated and reading from a tome.

The chapel also contains a narrow, spiral staircase which leads upwards into the Lord’s chambers above. The latest Lord Cheltingdon had this addition put it to facilitate his devotion to the faith.

1. Great Hall

This large, well lit room, serves as the beating heart of the manor. The Lord and his minions spend much of their time here, either receiving guests or petitioners, entertaining, or simply dining. The room serves many purposes, and it is not unusual to see bedrolls spread along the walls to accommodate overnight guests.

The ceiling is open to the second floor above, and the floor is dominated by a large hearth in the center of the room. A raised dias at one end serves as the Lord’s seat, from which he hears petitions and dispenses justice. Behind the dias, very intricate stained glass windows provide a colorful backdrop.

The room holds two long tables and many chairs, set for feasting. The tables can accommodate up to twenty diners, and others can be set up if more space is needed. When no guests are present, the Lord’s household dines more simply, at the table closest to the kitchen. Depending upon the formality of the occasion, the tables will be set with either simple place settings or with formal silver.

1. Kitchen

A large kitchen serves the needs of the manor. A large fire pit in one wall serves as the primary cooking space, and several cauldrons are always suspended above the heat. Central tables serve as a preparation area, and a series of cupboards along the wall contain all of the necessary tools of the cook’s trade.

It is overseen by a middle-aged, attractive woman by the name of Mellie Millar. She is an exacting task mistress and also serves as the head of the household. She has been with Lord Cheltingdon for many years and is something of a favorite of his. He values her advice on all matters, from the mundane running of the household to how to deal with tenants. She is remarkably well educated, having learned letters and numbers from a priest who once traveled with Lord Cheltingdon. Her loyalty to the Lord is unswerving and she views most outsiders with suspicion, assuming they seek to take advantage of the her master’s generous nature.

A plump, pleasant woman named Vera does the actual cooking. She produces tasty, if not particularly imaginative fare, and is known throughout the region as a kind soul willing to befriend anyone, regardless of station. All of the tenant farmers on the manor are at least passing acquaintances, and Vera’s friendship and generosity towards the locals has resulted in the Lord’s table always having the freshest of foodstuffs.

1. Mellie’s Room

As Lord Drakeson’s oldest and closest retainer, Mellie Millar has the privilege of private quarters off of the kitchen. The room is modest, but palatial by the standards of other household staff in other manors. The room contains a simple bed, a desk and chair (from where Mellie keeps the Manor’s books), and an armoire containing Mellie’s personal belongings.

Although there is generally little of value in the room, a careful search will turn up a loose section of floor within which a small cashbox is hidden. This is the petty cash for the manor, and Mellie has its contents memorized, down to the smallest coin. The box contains $200 in mostly small denomination coins.

1. Pantry

This storeroom contains most of the necessities for life in the manor house. Boxes and bags of foodstuffs and barrels and casks of ale, wine, and spirits line the walls. Cupboards hold smaller items, tools, utensils, spices, and similar odds and ends.

1. Gallery

At the top of the stairs on the second floor is an open gallery. One one side, the room

is bordered by a waist-high railing, to prevent accidental tumbles to the floor of the Great Hall some twelve feet below. In theory, one could sit or stand at the railing and observe the revelries taking place in the Great Hall.
