In practice, one’s enjoyment of the scene below tends to be marred by the smoke rising from the central hearth below which can be quite intense along the railing.

The gallery also functions as a central location for informal socializing. The room contains several couches for sitting, as well as a table and chairs. When the manor is not entertaining guests, the Lord and Lady of the house will often take their meals here. The walls are decorated with paintings and tapestries, and several suits of armor stand along the walls. These suits are clearly well-worn, and were actually used by Lord Cheltingdon during his illustrious career.

This area effectively functions as the family’s central living area and as the primary center for entertaining close friends, for whom the Great Hall would be overdoing it.

1. Lord’s Sitting Room

The Lord’s chambers are split into two sections, separated by a thick curtain. In the sitting area, the Lord’s private stair rises from the Chapel below. The room also contains a small table with three comfortable chairs, bookcases, and another suit of Lord Cheltingdon’s old armor. The walls are adorned with various heraldic hand weapons including swords, maces, and a particularly nasty looking battle axe.

An elaborate carpet graces the middle of the floor, obviously of masterful workmanship. Unbeknownst to any but Lord Cheltingdon, it is also the knight’s emergency escape plan, as it is an enchanted Carpet of Flying which he acquired during his adventuring days.

1. Solar

The Lord’s sleeping area, sometimes called the Solar, is separated from the sitting area by a thick red curtain. The curtain is usually left partially open when the Lord is not abed, so as to allow the light from the three large windows in the Solar to penetrate the sitting area.

This area simply contains the Lord and Lady’s bed, more bookcases, a very expensive looking mirror and a couple of armoires holding clothing.

1. Privy

Although the rest of the household has to trek out to the facilities outside, the Lord has a small privy located off of his quarters. The simple facilities include primitive

plumbing which sees waste carried down into the cellar where it is regularly collected and emptied by the staff. A small bucket of water kept in the privy allows for limited “flushing”. Incense is usually kept burning in this room to avoid odors.

1. Guest Room

This room is generally kept in readiness for any distinguished visitors who might attend the manor. It is simply furnished with a bed, desk, and dressers, and is reserved for other nobles or clergy who might stop for the night. Less distinguished guests sleep in the Great Hall.

1. Sons’ Room

Lord Cheltingdon has two teenaged sons who share this room. The eldest, Marke, will leave next year to serve as a squire in the home of another noble. The Baron of Greensdorf, a close family friend, has agreed to take in the boy. Marke Drakeson is very eager to leave, feeling that his father has been overprotective.

The younger son, Devin, has mixed feelings about Marke’s departure. On the one hand, it will be wonderful to have a room to himself, but given the closeness of the two brothers, he will certainly miss his sibling. Unlike Marke, Devin has shown no interest in knightly pursuits and Lord Cheltingdon is considering sending the boy into the service of the gods when he comes of age for apprenticeship.

The room is generally untidy, given the nature of the boys who occupy it. It is sparsely furnished with two beds, two dressers and a couple of bench-seats along the walls.

1. Squire’s Room

As is the tradition, Lord Cheltingdon has taken in the eldest son of his friend Lord Bardwill as his squire. Squire Goran has been with the household for two years now, and is well-liked by the family. He is less beloved by the staff, as his attitudes are more conservative than Lord Cheltingdon’s, and he tends to flaunt his status over the commoners in and around the manor.

Like the other bedrooms, the squire’s room is Spartan, with only a couple of dressers and chairs in addition to the simple bed.

## Money Changer

**Role in the Campaign**

Adventurers, by the nature of their profession, acquire large amounts of disposable wealth. Depending upon the style of campaign being run, this wealth can take the form of coinage, gems, jewels, or various types of less portable and less-easily exchanged items of worth. For example, in a campaign with a Viking-style flavor, successful adventurers could return home with a small amount of silver coins, a variety of household goods and livestock, a sack full of religious icons of precious metals, or even slaves.

An added complication to the transport and use of wealth is the fact that in many campaigns, adventuring parties travel across great distances and may run into situations where the coins they looted from the latest treasure trove are not recognized or accepted by merchants in far off lands. The transmission of wealth (be it coins or merchandise) into locally acceptable forms of currency, is the primary role of the money changer.

Usually only found in urban centers, the money changer’s shop is often a familiar stop for adventurers. Although they may gripe about the commissions, or questions the appraisal of certain goods by the money changer, they usually cannot avoid the need for this service. In larger cities, competition will usually keep the money changer relatively

honest, but be aware that exchange rates may be fixed by local guilds to maximize the profit of their members.

Rates and commissions may vary dramatically depending upon a number of factors. As a general rule, coinage may be exchanged for between 75% and 90% of its original value. Particularly obscure currency may be worth even less, on a case-by-case basis, depending upon the difficulty the merchant will have in turning it over himself. The following table offers some guidelines to rates charged by a money-changer to buy various mundane treasures for local coin. Add up any factors which apply, and subtract the result from 100% to determine the value offered for the exchange.

Low commission 5%

Average commission 10%

High commission 15%

Usurious commission 20% Coinage of an adjacent realm 5% Coinage of a distant realm 10% Coinage of a remote realm 20% Gems or jewels 10%

Artwork 15%

Example: If an adventurer seeks to turn 100 dwarven gold coins into local currency in a pirate city on another continent, he would receive 60 equivalent local coins, based on a 60% exchange rate (100% minus the usurious commission of 20% – they are pirates after all – and a 20% loss due to the coinage being from a remote realm).

Adventurers seeking to turn their local coin into a more obscure form of currency (in anticipation of a trip to another realm for example) can make the exchange for equivalent value foreign coins at face value minus the appropriate level of commission. This assumes that the money-changer has the necessary currency on hand of course. The chance that a particular currency is in stock can be estimated as follows:

Coinage of an adjacent realm: 80% Coinage of a distant realm: 50% Coinage of a remote realm: 10%

Money-changers, by their nature, are often the targets of thieves or other organized forces that seek to separate them from their wealth (taxmen, religious organizations,

lawyers…) As such they tend to be quite well protected physically, via guards, wards, and traps. They also are likely to be quite well connected to local power centers, either legitimate authorities or underworld bosses. Many will in fact pay off or cultivate relations with both these groups to provide a counter-weight, one to the other.

In some societies, money-changers may also function as money-lenders. Money- lenders are covered in a separate entry in this book, but the link should be noted. While money-lending is often a reviled (if necessary) profession, it is worth noting that a similar stigma usually does not attach itself to the money-changer. Money-changers are often among the wealthiest class of merchant in larger cities.

## Variations on the theme

The money changer has been caught in the crossfire of a “business dispute” between two rival criminal gangs. After years of trying to remain neutral by paying off both sides, he now finds both sides believing him to be supporting the other. He is desperate to find someone to escort his daughters to a distant city.

The party’s latest haul included some very rare, ancient coins. The local money changer is also something of an amateur archeologist and wants to accompany the party back to the location of their find to discover for himself if there is any other sign of the long defunct empire that struck the coins.

A local evil cult has set up a money changer’s shop in the city with the intention of handing out coins tainted with the evil essence of their deity/master/demonic presence. These coins are spreading across the city, insinuating dark thoughts and emotions into those who handle them. The rate of violent crime has shot up across the city as the evil works its way into the minds of the populace.

## Gimble’s Coin Emporium

((begin text to be read to players))

Finding a money-changer has not proven difficult at all. Located just inside the main city gate, the shop is gaudily painted in various unrelated bright colors. Intended to attract the eye of incoming merchants, the tall, boldly worded sign proclaims to one an all, “Gimble’s Coin Emporium – Reasonable Rates”.

((end player text))

## Overview

Gimble is the quintessential entrepreneur. He arrived in the big city and immediately set out to make himself rich. Unsuited for a life of crime by his inherent cowardice, Gimble looked to the life of the merchant class to make his way in the world. He dabbled in a variety of goods, opening first a carting business, then a general store and mortuary. After inheriting a small sum of money, Gimble decided that the best way to make money was to, in a manner of speaking, sell money. Thus he has established the Coin Emporium as his lastest venture.

A colorful member of the local merchant’s guild, Gimble is widely known throughout the city for being willing to cut a deal with anyone, and for not being particular as to where the coinage to be converted originated. His indifferent moral focus makes him a popular confederate of some of the less honest folk in town. While his involvement in criminal activity is usually limited to laundering their ill-gotten gains (exchanging stolen Dwarven Sovereigns for a less traceable coinage for example), he is also sometimes willing to purchase other items “acquired informally”.

Gimble’s saving grace is that while he is willing to buy and sell to anyone without asking too many questions, he does actually deal honestly with his own customers. His rates are quite reasonable (falling squarely into the average commission rate of around 10%) and he is often willing to haggle.

## Shop Exterior

Gimble’s Coin Emporium is a very sturdy structure, as one might well expect from an establishment within the walls of which so much money is kept. The walls are built of thick stone and the ground floor is windowless. An iron-bound thick wooden door serves as the only obvious entrance into the shop.

The stern, imposing nature of the structure is offset by the almost random selection of paints which have been splashed across the walls. Although some local merchants wrinkle their noses in disdain at what they deem Gimble’s lack of “seriousness” as a

businessman, they cannot argue with his success. Faced with stiff competition, Gimble has deliberately set out to ensure that his shop stands out. In this he has succeeded as no one enters this city gate without marveling at the spectacle.

## Shop Interior

1. Door

The heavy iron-bound door is securely barred from the inside at all times. If a customer desires to enter to do business, they use the heavy iron knocker to announce their presence. After a brief delay, a small barred window in the door will be opened and one of Gimble’s guards will politely enquire as to the visitor’s business. If he deems the answer acceptable, he will unbar the door and allow the customer into the shop.

1. Customer Area

Most of the shop remains off limits to customers as Gimble chooses to do business in this cramped, simple room. A small round table and three chairs serve as Gimble’s place of business.

When the door is opened to admit a customer, Gimble will be comfortably seated in a plush armchair behind one side of the table. Customers will be offered seats – noticeably less comfortable than the one Gimble is seated in – and will be invited to conduct their business across the simple tabletop.

The guard who opened the door for the customers will re-bar the door after their entry and then take up a position in the corner behind the seated clients. He remains alert throughout the transaction and will intervene at the slightest hint of trouble.

A typical transaction involves Gimble examining the coinage or items that the customer wishes to exchange and calculating the exchange rate and commission. Assuming the customer agrees to the proposed exchange amount, Gimble will take the coins or goods and pass them to another guard through a small hatch in the inner door. The guard will then retrieve the agreed to amount of money and pass it back to Gimble.

1. Inner Shop

The small customer area is closed off from the rest of the shop by another heavy door with a small pass-through hatch. Another guard is always on duty in the inner shop and alert for signs of disturbance in the customer area. This guard also functions as banker, depositing cash and items in the vault and passing funds out to Gimble in the

customer area.

1. Vault

This small room is where Gimble’s fortune is kept. The room is completely sealed except for an iron door which is secured by three separate, high quality locks (DC 24).

## Smithy

**Role in the Campaign**

As society becomes more specialized, most people have lost the knowledge necessary to perform simple tasks.	In more rural or less sophisticated societies, most

people may still retain the necessary skill to work a forge and transform metal into simple tools, but for the most part this task has passed to a specialist – the blacksmith.

Little can be done in the average fantasy milieu without metal. Everyone from farmers to townsfolk needs someone to manufacture the tools and equipment they have to have in order to survive another year. Whether they be mundane items like nails or horseshoes, or indeed more sophisticated items such as gears for a windmill or intricate tumblers for a lock, the blacksmith is usually the artisan for the job. As is so often the case in eras before formal education, the profession is usually passed down by masters to apprentices.

While the local blacksmith is generally capable of attempting the fabrication of any particular metal object, it should be noted that in many jurisdictions the local authorities strictly control the crafting of arms and armor. At any rate, the production of fine weapons and armor is an art form that very few blacksmiths have the inclination (or time) to develop. The expensive raw materials for such work are usually beyond the means of the humble village smith and few have the leisure to learn such work without a paying commission.

Unlike horseshoes or axeheads, there is very little demand for plate armor in the average peasant village. The local blacksmith can usually turn out the necessary items for the local militia, be it round helms, spear points, or functional knives, but he will usually lack the ability to fashion unusual or complex weapons unless he has been mandated to support the local Lord’s forces. It is worth noting that many noble households count within their staff a specialized smith, armorer, or weapon crafter devoted to keeping the Lord’s forces armed.

For adventurers, the smith may still be an important ally as in the absence of an accredited armorer he might still be able to make temporary or makeshift repairs to damaged items even if he would generally not be able to produce them himself.

The local smith may also make adventurers a staunch ally in the community due to his central role in the life of the town or village. The smithy is generally a mainstay of communities of almost any size. The constant need to make to repair a wide variety of important tools makes it impractical for people to travel very far to acquire the services of a blacksmith. The local smith will generally be a well-respected member of the local community, often serving a leading role among the local peasantry. Being by nature strong and hardworking individuals, they are often deferred to by other locals and may well occupy a position of local authority.

Usually centrally located, the smithy is a busy, bustling place. Most smiths will have several apprentices on hand to assist with the work. If possible, these apprentices should be at different stages in their learning so that the smith can assign different duties to each. Often a mid-stage apprentice will produce the more mundane items at a smithy, nails. New apprentices will simply assist the smith with his work – operating the bellows for example – while senior apprentices would craft more sophisticated items.

## Variations on the Theme

The local smith has an inquisitive mindset and may be interested in any strange or unusual metals or minerals that adventurers may acquire. Finding a new source for more well-known, but still rare, materials may also endear a party to the local smith. If there are rumors of such materials being available, the smith may want to commission a party to seek them out.

The local smith might also be a former adventurer. Warriors who have given up the adventuring life might well choose to settle down as smiths. Their strength lends itself to such a profession. As a former adventurer, the smith may know of local areas of interest, or may seekout a party to accompany him back to the scene of his greatest defeat to recover the remains of his comrades. His conscience has long troubled him over his failure to save his friends, and he seeks to make this right.

The smithy is controlled by local law and custom and is forbidden, under pain of death, to provide service to outlanders without a special permit. If the party is in dire need to the services of a smith, they may need to approach the local Lord to get approval, and he may require them to perform some service in exchange. Alternately, they might appeal to the smith to flout the law, either for money or due to the noble nature of the cause.

## Sample – The Rusty Nail

((Text to be read to players))

The ringing of the hammer against the anvil leaves no doubt that you have arrived. The smithy has a competent, ordered look about it and the smith is hard at work on what appears to be the head of an axe. This village may not be much, but at least it has a blacksmith worthy of the name.

((end player text))

## Overview

The Rusty Nail could be any blacksmith shop in any village. It holds no mystical portals or unknown catacombs. It does, however, hold one of the keys to the survival of any rural village – the secret of working metal. Gulliver Dornal, known only half in jest as Gulliver Hammerhand to his local cronies, has been a blacksmith for over thirty years, and is quite proficient in the fashioning of mundane metal objects.

Although the local Lord would willingly grant him a charter to make and sell some weapons and armor, Gulliver does not and will not work or such items. A fervent pacifist, he will neither make nor mend any item of war. Gulliver has long held this belief and even will never willingly put his hand to such work. If coerced into working on such equipment, Gulliver will protest continually, and if the coercion persists he will reluctantly undertake the job. He will, however do a deliberately poor job, and he will intentionally introduce flaws into the work such that the item repaired has double the normal chance to fail or break in combat.

The smithy is a small, simple building divided between Gulliver’s shop and his living quarters. He lives alone, his wife having died five years ago from a particularly virulent strain of Goblin Pox that struck the region. Although Gulliver is still a vigorous and handsome man, he has shown no interest in remarrying, despite the not-so-subtle hints of one of the local widows. He is content with his current lot in life and places great emphasis on passing his knowledge down to his sole remaining apprentice, a promising young woman named Rezellda.

Though it is unusual to see a woman in the role of a smith, Rezellda has silenced any murmurs or snickers through her diligent work, her serious attitude, and through a couple of well-placed thrashings of particularly obnoxious young men. She is tall, muscular, intelligent, and completely devoted to Gulliver and his shop. Though tongues do still wag after a few ales too many, no one with a strong attachment to their teeth is foolish enough to suggest that anything improper occurs between the smith and his apprentice.

## Shop Exterior

The shop is solidly built of wood with a well-shingled roof. The windows have sturdy shutters and the doors appear squarely hung. All in all, the shop give the impression of being well maintained by a competent workman.

Beside the shop is a small courtyard area where Gulliver’s forge can be found next

to a well. The forge and anvil are the most likely locations to find Gulliver during the day. Also located in the courtyard is a large stack of wood, used to feed the forge and hearth. The stump of a large tree serves as a splitting block.

The area around the forge also serves as something of a hangout for several of Gulliver’s friends. Chances are, if there is gossip to be heard, someone at the forge will be more than willing to pass it on. This has lead to a local expression, “I heard it at the forge”, which is understood to mean that one has confidence in the accuracy of a statement. At any give time during the day there is a 75% chance that 1d3 townsfolk will be lounging near the forge.

## Shop Interior

The interior of the shop is simple in the extreme. Basic wood paneled walls and a rough wood floor frame Gulliver’s few possessions. Over half of the building is given over to the smithy, while the rest serves as living quarters.

Shop

1. The Forge

As mentioned above, the forge and anvil stand in a small courtyard just outside the shop door. Patrons must pass by the forge to enter the shop, which Gulliver feels gives them a chance to examine his work. He is very proud of his forge, newly built after the old one needed replacing. Like the rest of the building, the forge was built by hand by Gulliver and his general competence as a tradesman shows in the care with which the work was completed.

A deep well stand beside the forge and Gulliver invites any who wish to draw water from it. There are invariably numerous buckets of water standing around the forge for the smith’s use, but drinking from these would be ill-advised.

1. Hearth

A large firepit dominates the shop, and there is always a steady supply of glowing coals. The heart is actually the remnant of the old forge, which Gulliver continues to use for small, simple tasks. He keeps it going largely out of sentimentality, but is coming to realize that the new forge is really all he needs.

The hearth is open and could be dangerous to anyone not paying attention to where they walk. An unexpected tumble into the coals could definitely ruin someone’s day!

1. Workspace

Gulliver uses these two high tables for much of his work. The wood tops are scarred and singed from years of hard use, and they are usually covered with tools and various projects that Gulliver undertakes on his own time. He is constantly tinkering with designs, seeking new ways to make standard items. He is currently pondering ways to make common farming implements more resistant to rust. Thus far his experiments have been disappointments.

1. Tools

An impressive display of common, uncommon, rare, and even unique tools take up one entire wall of the shop. Gulliver is something of a packrat when it comes to tools, and his late wife often complained that he buys more than he sells. The quickest way to his heart is to present him with a new tool or a variation on a tool that he has never seen.

The racks on the wall are filled with odd shaped hammers, saws, planes, tongs, pry bars, and the like. They also contain more obscure items, like a Gnomish Smoke Shifter, a left-handed can opener, a mechanized metal punch, and a Dwarven whetstone that Gulliver believes to be magical.

Living Area

1. Bed

Gulliver maintains a tidy living area, with his small bed always neatly made. Although previously he was somewhat untidy, much to the dismay of his wife, since her death he has unconsciously tried to keep the house as tidy as she would have like.

A small chest beside the bed holds Gulliver’s modest valuables. It has a very intricate lock (DC 35) that Gulliver purchased from a traveling tinker. The chest contains a small sum of money (coins with a total value of $30) as well as a few mementos of his late wife.

1. Stove

The house holds a self-contained wood-burning stove of Gulliver’s own design. It was something of a prototype and works very well. Gulliver’s wife considered it a significant improvement over cooking over an open fire pit. Although such stoves are not uncommon, this one was designed to increase the efficiency of the wood burning such that it uses about half the wood of conventional stoves.

The stove includes a special drawer underneath where various pots and other

cooking implements can be kept. A small sitting and eating area to one side serves as Gulliver’s dining area. Rezellda often eats with him in the evenings, as will any of his cronies still around by then.

1. Armor

Some might think that a suit of armor is out of place in the home of a pacifist, even if he is a blacksmith. Indeed, Gulliver has placed the armor here as a joke. It is intended to impart to visitors the futility of violence.

The suit of armor is an exquisitely made set of plate armor, obviously crafted by a master. Intricate engravings cover most surfaces, and the steel is burnished to a high gloss. All in all it is a very impressive piece of martial kit, no doubt treasured by the knight who commissioned the suit. Unfortunately, the effect is ruined by the ragged, six-inch wide hole that has been blasted through the center of the breastplate and out the back of the backplate, obviously by some form of magic. Clearly, whoever was unfortunate enough to be wearing this armor on that day was nearly cut in half by the force.

Gulliver keeps this armor as a symbol of his refusal to make implements of war.

## D20 Game Information

Gulliver Dornal, Male Human Exp9: CR 8; Medium Humanoid ; hp 35; Fort +4, Ref +2, Will +7;

STR 16, DEX 9, CON 13, INT 12, WIS 13, CHA 11.

Skills: Appraise +10, Craft (Blacksmithing) +11, Craft (Carpentry) +8, Craft (Leatherworking) +6, Craft (Locksmithing) +10.

## Trading Post

**Role in the Campaign**

In most fantasy campaigns, much of the adventure takes places beyond the borders of the so-called civilized lands. While adventures do occur in more urban settings, the traditional quests for lost tombs and lairs of vile beasts still tend to be set in wilder, less settled areas.

In many cases, the remoteness of the location will pose something of a problem to adventurers in search of supplies. Small homesteads or mining camps will rarely have the kind of gear sought after by professional adventurers, and more importantly will not be interested in purchasing or trading for the various trinkets and baubles that adventurers might haul out of the depths of some dank tomb. Into this void comes the trading post.

Much like the general store in a larger urban setting, the trading post is a multi- purpose business. Often these businesses are part store, part tavern, part post-office, and part community center. For those hardy souls who spend their lives on the frontier, a trip to the trading post is often a much anticipated break from the routine of their lives. Generations of locals likely have met weekly at the local post, exchanged news, griped about the weather, or taxes, or marauding trolls, and sneered at itinerant adventurers.

When adventurers first arrive in a new rural locale, it behooves them to seek out a reliable source for equipment, and trading posts are generally good places to obtain hard to find items. Chances are, if the post doesn’t have a mundane bit of adventuring gear, it can get it – though delivery of the item may take time.

The hardy souls who set off to open trading posts in the more remote corners of the world are not shy, diffident storekeepers. They are often former adventurers themselves who enjoy the steady contact with members of their former profession. Regardless of their past experience, they are almost invariably tough individualists who are willing and capable of defending their small business. Given the nature of most campaign worlds, trading posts will tend to be easily defensible, fortified places.

## Variations on the theme

Contrary to the norm, a naïve, sickly-looking young man and his beautiful young bride run the trading post the party has arrived at. They have come to the frontier in search of a new life and haven’t the slightest idea of the dangers they face. They are still unpacking their wagon when the party first arrives, and honorable adventurers will be hard pressed to leave these newcomers to their obvious fate…

The party may stumble upon a trading post which offers bounties on the heads of various local nuisance humanoids or monsters. Goblins, orcs, trolls, and similar creatures are good candidates for bounties. The party may view this as “free” money, since they are likely to be routinely dispatching such foes anyway, but if the adventurers just happen to be at the trading post when the local monsters decide its time for some

payback, a nasty battle could develop.

Trading posts needn’t deal solely in the mundane. A wizard might well set up a trading post in a region where particularly rare or valuable magical components might be found, and do a brisk business trading mundane items and equipment for gems, herbs, flowers, insects, body parts, or other things with arcane potential.

## Toll Road/Bridge

**NOTE: The floorplan for this locale was done at a scale of 1 square = 10 feet – different than all the others!**

## Role in the Campaign

Those who travel the roads and rivers of most fantasy worlds can readily attest to the truth of the old saying the nothing in this life is free. In particular, the use of roads and byways is rarely without costs. Whether those costs need be paid in coin or blood is not always clear.

In most cases those demanding tolls for the use of roads, bridges and fords, will be representatives of the legitimate ruler of the region. After all, it costs money to build, maintain, and patrol the roads, and surely those costs ought to be borne by those who benefit from the infrastructure. These tolls are rarely prohibitive as most local authorities have learned that given enough financial incentive, merchants and other travelers will find other ways to get to their destination. The trick is finding a toll that enriches the toll-taker without being inconvenient enough to drive business away.

While individual tolls might not prove too much of a burden, in particularly populated areas they can become a nuisance. Areas in which control of the lands is particularly fragmented or subinfeudated may result in travelers being stopped every few miles to pay another toll. The financial costs will be unlikely to seriously inconvenience any but the most impoverished adventuring party, but heroes in a hurry may find the delays on busy roads to be maddening.

In more remote areas, away from any lawful force that might frown upon the

practice, tolls may be collected by anyone with the arm strength to enforce them. Whether this be a band of woodland merry men or a powerful individual or monster, these tolls are often more painful than the legitimate type and are generally indistinguishable from outright theft.

DMs should be creative in thinking about tolls. Not all treasures sought by the toll taker are monetary. A particularly nasty undead creature might haunt a river ford where it drowned and demand to drain some lifeforce (or even an entire life) in exchange for passage. A mysterious knight might insist on a test of arms before anyone can cross the bridge. A leprechaun might expect an answer to a riddle.

## Variations on the theme

An adventuring party on a particularly time sensitive quest may find an encounter with a particularly officious toll taker to be infuriating. Make sure the small, bumbling bureaucrat in charge of the station is backed up by the full weight of the local authorities (making it less likely that more chaotic characters will simply hack their way through). Invent any number of regulations that require special tolls or paperwork to drag out the encounter. “Oh, you’re a Half-Orc. Let me see, I think that requires Form H8… Yes, here it is… Oh my, there’s a different algorithmic table of tolls for Half-Orcs. Let me get my abacus…” To add insult to injury, insist in payment in an obscure local currency – exact change only.

Characters arriving at a toll station discover the station manager and all his attendants dead. As they are investigating the scene, a strong force of the King’s cavalry arrives and takes them for murdering bandits…

A monastery of spiritually pure, but obnoxiously self-righteous monks have established a toll booth of sorts across a mountain pass that the party simply must get through. Not interested in monetary tolls, the monks insist that anyone passing through must agree to perform a service “for the good of all”. Creative parties might think up relatively impressive sounding, but not very taxing, services.

## The Troll Bridge

(Text to be read to players)

_As the road winds towards the river, you can clearly see ahead of you a sturdy, three-span stone bridge. The water is high and running fast, no doubt from the recent rains, as the omnipresent dark clouds serve to remind you. A giant-sized cottage stands beside the bridge, smoke curling lazily from the chimney. In front of the house, a tidy, tended garden plot shows signs of careful attention. A faint whiff of baking bread stirs your senses, perhaps aided by the memory of the depressing number of travel rations you’ve recently consumed._

_The bridge appears at first glance to be just like any other stone bridge you have seen recently; perhaps a little old and run-down, but apparently structurally sound and sturdy. What is different, at least for a bridge this far away from a town or even a village, is the large cottage standing at the near end of the bridge. Other than its unusual size – the door is some 10 feet tall_

_-- It would not be out of place in a quiet country thorp, or outside the walls of a baronial manor, but here, unprotected by liege or law, it defies sense that such an unfortified structure should survive the depredations of predators._

_At the side of the road a crudely lettered sign has been affixed to a post, sagging to one side as it dangles from the single nail: Troll Bridge._

(End players’ text)

## Overview

Beyond the frontiers of the civilized world, in the wilderness where there is no law save what one can assert by force of arms, many are the creatures and forces that find novel ways to amass wealth. Some will naturally seek to enrich themselves through outright theft and/or violence, but some find other ways to part travelers from their coin, albeit often with a thinly veiled threat of that same violence. If nobles and merchants can turn bridges into tollbooths, why should not a troll?

As the characters approach the troll bridge, they will be confronted by a very odd troll who will seek to charge them a toll to cross his bridge. The troll is quite powerful, but has a strong distaste for physical confrontation. Although he will fight if cornered, he prefers to attempt to reason with travelers and, if necessary, will allow them to cross the bridge (at least part way). The center of the bridge is trapped, and the unwary could well find themselves bobbing in the river. Retrieving and looting drowned corpses is still easier than fighting to this troll’s way of thinking.

The party has arrived at the only bridge for many miles across a deep, fast-running

river. Arturus, an unusual troll, has claimed the bridge and makes a comfortable living exacting tolls from passers-by.

The bridge crosses a wide expanse of water, its three stone spans bearing the weight of the 250-foot long bridge. It is wide enough for one fully loaded wagon and sturdy and strong despite it looking a bit aged. It was built more than 150 years ago by dwarves, working on commission for a mage, the ruins of whose tower can be found about a mile down the road on the other side of the bridge. As most things built by and for wizards, the bridge has its own magical secret (see description of Arturus the Troll below).

It is built of mixed field-stones, and held together by marvelous dwarven engineering with nary a drop of mortar. It has clearly seen much use over the years as its smooth surface shows the ruts of much cart and wagon traffic. The roadway over the bridge is flanked on either side by a low stone wall, approximately three feet high, to prevent the careless or the inebriated from plunging unexpectedly into the water.

The river is currently up and running high due to recent rains, and there is a barely a five foot gap between the rushing current and the bottom of the bridge. The water roars angrily between the spans, swirling madly before calming once past the structure. The water is stirred up and murky, with much debris and not a few small tree trucks bobbing past the bridge.

## Cottage Exterior

The cottage is clearly built for giant-sized occupants. Its style and architecture, however, are standard human design. It is a well-built, sturdy-looking peasant home, made of simple wood frame construction and a surprisingly sophisticated tin roof. Someone has clearly gone to some trouble to build this home, as materials for the roof would have to have been brought in at great expense from a skilled metal smith.

It is neatly tended and brightly painted in blue with white trim. The front is punctuated by a large door and two windows. The shutters on the windows are flung wide to allow the fresh air in and the smell of baking out. Like most homes in this part of the world, the windows do not have glass in them, but are covered inside by colorful curtains.

To either side of the front door of the cottage, inside a neat wooden fence, are two raised flowerbeds, well tended and weed-free. The beds are covered in soft, wood-chip

mulch and the heavy clay soil sprouts colorful displays of violet delphiniums, yellow daylilies, and lavender hostas. On the east side of the house, another bed contains a variety of herbs and medicinal plants. Use the table below to determine the plants and their reputed properties. Note that these are merely the properties people believe them to have. Game Masters should determine what use, if any, a skilled herbalist could make of these plants in game terms.

(Boxed Text)

## Name	Usage	Folklore Property

01 Marigold	Fresh Flowers	Comprehend language of birds 02 Columbine	Fresh Flowers	Courage

1. Blackberry	Fresh Leaves	Healing
1. Bay	Dried Leaves	Removes hexes
1. CloverDried 4-Leaf Clovers	See invisible creatures 06 Lemon Balm Fresh Leaves	Love
1. Rose	Rose Hips	Good luck
1. Chamomile	Fresh Flowers		Luck with money 09 Garlic Fresh/Dried Bulb	Protection from vampires 10 Dill	Seeds		Protects children
1. Mugwort	Fresh/Dried Leaves Protection from poison
1. Raspberry	Wood	Protection from spirits
1. Dandelion	Head	Telepathy
1. Thyme	Fresh Leaves	Repel negative energy
1. Celery Seeds	Sleep
1. Geranium	Fresh Flower	Protection from snakes
1. Saffron	Dried Flower	Control winds
1. Rue	Fresh Sprig	Protection vs were-creatures
1. Sage	Fresh Leaves	Grants Wishes
1. Amaranth	Fresh Leaves	Invisibility (End boxed text)

## Cottage Interior

1. **Entryway**. Inside the cottage, visitors will find a neat living space, with sparse but stylish furnishings. Naturally, all of the furniture is also giant sized. Upon entering, visitors are greeted by a cheerfully lit scene as light pours in through the four windows. The colorfully sewn curtains are pulled back and restrained by small knit ropes to allow

the light and air to enter. In front of the door, a rectangular rug, apparently Elvish in make, greets visitors.

1. **Kitchen.** The kitchen area is tidy but obviously much used. Knives and other utensils are hung from hooks beside the hearth and the cooking fireplace. A counter along the south wall holds jars and bottles and boxes filled with spices, roots, vegetables, and other food stuffs. A loaf of bread, apparently freshly baked, sits on a wooden cutting board next to a slab of butter. An amphora of cold, clear water rests to one side of the kitchen table and a keg of decent Halfling cider stands on the other.

The table is apparently where most of the food preparation takes place, and it appears that the homeowner was in the midst of brewing a pot of herbal tea when the party arrived. A cast iron kettle rests on a flat stone tile, still very hot from the fire. A teapot stands ready to receive the hot water, and a jar of dried Lemon Balm leaves is open beside it.

1. **Eating Area.** To the east of the door, an enormous table and four chairs occupy the corner. The table stands some seven feet tall and the chairs are similarly massive. On top of the table rests a leather pouch containing pipeweed as well as a tankard of cider.

1. **Study.** In the northeast corner of the room, a utilitarian desk is cluttered with books, scrolls, papers, and jars of ink. A cursory inspection of the books will reveal that most of the works relate to historical dissertations on an ancient tribe of hunter- gatherers who once inhabited the local area. Closer inspection will reveal many passages apparently highlighted by the reader, dealing with the shamanistic spirit magic of these peoples. Finally, a sustained study of all the works and the highlighted passages will reveal that the reader was particularly interested in burial rites and related curses. The materials might prove valuable if the collection were taken to a large city where sages or loremasters might have an interest in their historical value. Also on the desk, hidden by the clutter, is a large key which opens the chest described in area 5.

1. **Chest.** Between the desk and the bed is a very large wooden chest. It is securely locked and will require a skilled hand to open without the key. Inside are a variety of troll-sized items of clothing, as well as some older, much smaller personal items. Someone schooled in matters related to clothing or materials might realize that these items are of gnomish make. A moneybox lies at the bottom of the chest, beneath all of the clothes and other personal items. It contains a variety of coins and other valuable items collected from travelers. Random coins from many different nations add up to

approximately $20000 in various denominations. A pearl necklace worth at least $3000 and several gold rings set with various stones, each valued at $1000, complete the collection.

1. **Sleeping Area.** A large bed, some twelve feet long, lies behind a curtain divider. The bed is neatly made, its two pillows freshly plumped and fluffed, with the comforter turned back at one corner. The mattress is a simple one stuffed with straw, but is comfortable and warm.

## Arturus the Troll

The cottage belongs to Arturus the troll, but things are not quite what they seem. Arturus started life as a gnome, and was an eager though inexperienced young thief when he made the mistake of seeking to pilfer the burial treasures of a tribal shaman’s tomb not far from the bridge. In centuries past, a nomadic tribe of animistic barbarians lived in these lands, and one of their most powerful shamans was put to rest in an elaborate cave tomb, well protected by magic and spirits.

Arturus’ attempt to break into the tomb triggered several magical traps and cost his companions their lives. Arturus escaped with his life, but suffered the effects of a powerful curse. Over the course of six painful days, he slowly transformed into a hideous troll. When the metamorphosis was complete, he was surprised to find his mind intact. Though physically a troll, he retains his uniquely gnomish personality.

For the past twelve years, Arturus has lived beside the bridge and amassed wealth by charging travelers for the privilege of crossing “his” bridge. When travelers arrive, he will come out of the house and greet them politely, welcoming them to his home. He will chat amicably before getting around to mentioning the toll. Arturus has no set toll. He asks travelers to donate what they think is “fair”. He will become angry if clearly offered less than the party can actually afford. He will make this judgment based upon the party’s manner and appearance. (i.e. He will be suspicious of an offer of a few copper pennies from anyone riding an expensive horse or wearing fine clothes). In general, he expects every traveler to turn over coins or items of considerable value.

Arturus is heroically greedy, but despite his fearsome appearance and powerful abilities, still a noticeably cowardly little gnome at heart. He will reason with travelers, pointing out that he maintains the bridge and keeps the area free of dangerous monsters, and that it is only fair that he be compensated. If that fails he will threaten and cajole and pose, but if confronted with combat he use his magic ring to turn

invisible and retreat. He will not go far, however, for he has not built his comfortable little homestead solely on the gold of those too timid to challenge him. Although Arturus prefers to deal with his “customers” in a peaceful and businesslike fashion, if crossed he is quite willing to use the magic of the bridge to waylay those who seek to cheat him.

When the bridge was built, the river saw a significant amount of boat traffic and the wizard worked with the dwarven builders to construct a very special kind of bridge which could accommodate the both land and river traffic. When the command word is spoken, the center 40-foot section of the bridge will vanish instantly, allowing tall boats to pass. This has the added benefit of dropping anyone standing on it into the churning river below. On more than one occasion, Arturus has taken his fee from the drowned body of a traveler. Anyone on the span when it vanishes gets one try to leap to safety or to grab the closest edge of the bridge before they fall into the water. (Reflex Save DC 18/Disaster Avoidance 3X Speed)

Should travelers engage him in conversation and show an interest in his apparently non-troll-like nature, he will invite them in and spend some time visiting with them. He will provide a good, hearty meal and after a few drinks may tell them his story if they appear appropriately sympathetic. Arturus has never stopped seeking a cure to his curse, and will offer the party his collected treasure, including his magic ring, should anyone be able to help break the curse. (Note: This should not be a simple matter of casting a spell on him. One possible way to help Arturus would be for the party seek out the Shaman’s tomb and explore it fully. Once the undead spirit of the Shaman is defeated, the curse would be lifted.)

Should the party offer up sufficient treasure to buy passage, Arturus wishes them well and assures them that the toll is good for the return trip. He only collects from traveler heading in one direction. He does not admit that this is because so few ever return from the lands across the river…

## 

**Reforged C&C Stats**

**Arturus**: male troll (Giant 6/Rogue 2), HP 93; AC 18; lawful evil; Primary attributes: Strength, Constitution, Dexterity; Significant attributes: Strength 23, Constitution 23, Dexterity 14, Intelligence 12, Wisdom 14, Charisma 10; Natural attacks: two claws (1d6+6) and a bite (1d6+3); Equipment: _bag of holding_ (Bag 1 – 250 pounds), _bracers of armor +2_, _ring of invisibility_ (grants invisibility at will).

## Audience Chamber

**Role in the Campaign**

Where one finds powerful men and women, one will find those with less power vying for their attention and favors. Whether the power in question belongs to a King, a lesser noble, a merchant prince, a powerful mage, a jealous godling, or a ruthless cutthroat, almost all of these will have a room or hall in which to meet and lord over their supplicants. The audience chamber is a place where many adventuring parties will find themselves, seeking either work, reward, or mercy.

It is often the custom for local authority figures to set aside a regular block of time, be it an hour or a day each week, to hear petitions from those subject to their power. Such audiences can range from very formal appearances before a court to simple meetings between the liege and the underling. In many cases, a special room will be set aside to hear petitions.

Audience chambers are often a reflection of the power that holds them. They can be austere, serious places in which the quiet dignity of the power hosting the audience dominates the room. They can be extravagant, seeking to overwhelm petitioners with awe and majesty as they gape at the gaudy display of wealth and power. In some cases, usually associated with extralegal power figures, they can be dark and hidden. The local assassins guildmaster is unlikely to greet petitioners in a rose garden, although just such a setting might put the unwary off their guard…

Audience chambers belonging to the local ruler or legitimate powers tend to be more formal and are usually very busy locales, with lines of potential petitioners gathering early in the morning in the hopes of having their request heard. The person hearing the petitions is invariably surrounded by advisors, guards, servants, and other members of the household, ensuring that the rabble have no chance to harm or otherwise threaten him.

## Variations on the theme

In some Kingdoms, the right to petition must be won. Each month, a tournament will be held in which the winners of several different events will be given the opportunity to petition the Queen. Some events might be martial – jousting, archery, or grand melee – while some might be more artistic – poetry, singing, or tale telling – or even mundane – plowing or ale brewing. Whatever the make up of the contests, some desperate petitioners might seek to hire professionals to compete on their behalf, hoping to have their petition carried forward.

Another possible variation is the religious audience, in which members of the faith are encouraged to come forward during one of the local holy days to seek counsel or aid from the clergy. The only catch is that the petition must be made in front of the entire congregation, and it must be the will of the gathered worshippers that the petition be granted.

Some authority figures will insist on payment for any services rendered to a petitioner. Perhaps in exchange for the use of one of the manorial oxen, a peasant might be expected to work an extra week on the Lord’s fields. Or an adventuring party, petitioning for access to the Duke’s library where a crucial map is believe to be, might be asked to first find out exactly what has been eating people in the depths of the local wood.

## Sample: Underworld Audience Chamber

This audience chamber belongs to a petty urban crime lord, but could easily be adapted to any other powerful figure such as a feudal nobleman or religious leader. The room serves as a place for petitioners to meet with the leader and seek favors.

((text to be read to players))

_The room before you is long and dim. A single bright light shines into your eyes from the far end of the room making it impossible to truly see who or what is waiting for you. As you stand conspicuously in the middle of the room, blinking into the light, you can sense your host already_

_sizing you up and taking your measure._

((end of player text))

## Overview

Just as there are different types of nobles, each with their own domains and varying degrees of power, so too does the underworld of the urban center have its own division of power. The aristocracy of crime is no less varied and no less jealous of its prerogative than its noble counterpart. Those seeking a favor unlikely to be granted by any law- abiding authority often find their way to these criminal lords and make obeisance to them just as sincerely as their true liege lord. Just such a need for extra-legal assistance might well bring characters into the audience chamber of Slink, leader of a small but sophisticated urban gang.

Slink takes his role as self-appointed overseer of his domain seriously, and has long recognized the value in being seen to “give back” to the community. He regularly sets aside time for common folk to petition him for assistance, usually in response to some injustice done to them by a merchant or noble. Although it is common knowledge that these audiences take place, it is difficult, if not impossible to find anyone who will admit to ever having sought one. Word on the street is that petitioners make themselves known at one of a number of taverns run by Slink’s partners, and then wait for word on where and when to present themselves. This summons usually leads them to a remote or obscure location where they are then blindfolded and taken to one of Slink’s hideouts, where his audience chamber awaits.

## Interior

Visitors are led, still blindfolded, down a narrow set of stairs to a solid wooden door. They are led into a small antechamber where their blindfolds are removed and where they wait with their guide until summoned into the audience chamber. The room is sparsely furnished and dimly lit by large candelabras in each corner. Six wooden chairs are scattered around the room for those unwilling to stand.

The guide who brought them to the room is known as Archie Five-Fingers, after his favorite type of merchant’s discount. A petty thief, he has the redeeming feature of being unerringly loyal to Slink ever since “the boss” got him out of a jam with a local wizard. Archie isn’t very bright, but his orders are simple. He is to bring visitors to the antechamber and keep them there until the inner door is opened to admit the petitioners. He takes this job very seriously and will not allow visitors to leave until after they have seen Slink. Anyone trying to leave before the audience will be

physically prevented from leaving and if necessary Archie will yell for help from a waiting squad of goons at the top of the stairs.

Archie is a suspicious sort, but he has a soft spot for women and priests, and will slowly open up should one of these groups appear sympathetic and interested in him. Under no circumstances however will he betray Slink.

When Slink and his lieutenants are ready to receive the party, the inner door will open and another member of Slink’s gang will beckon to the party to enter. Archie will remain in the antechamber. Looking through the doorway into the next room, it will be apparent that the audience chamber is poorly lit except for two bright light sources behind a raised dias. The result is that it is impossible to see with any detail who or what is seated on the dias. The fact that someone is there will be evident from the voice which will call out to the visitors, “Enter and speak. My time is valuable”.

The room is rectangular and lit by candles in wall sconces. Squinting into the bright light will allow the visitor to make out what appears to be several people seated on the dias. It is impossible to make out any details of these mysterious figures or to recognize any of their features.

The room is clearly furnished to make an impression. Despite the dim light, it is easy to see that the contents of the room are of high quality and very valuable. Side tables, comfortable chairs, fine art, and rows of finely bound books line both sides of the room. The contents of the room tend to change with time, as various stolen goods are stored here until an appropriate buyer can be found.

Visitors will be led forward to stand before the dias which is raised some four feet off of the ground. Looking up into the light, it will remain impossible to make out any details of the group on the dias.

In fact, Slink is accompanied by two close confidants, Norbin and Fatty Nolan. This triumvirate makes most of the important decisions regarding the status of the gang’s turf, though Slink always has the last word. Norbin is a large man whose size belies his quick wit. Fatty, on the other hand, is a gnome of reputed magical ability and prodigious appetite. Slink sits to the extreme left of the group, with Norbin sitting in the middle. Slink never speaks during audiences, leaving it to Fatty to communicate the leader’s will. After hearing a petition they will briefly confer as to whether to agree to the request and what the cost of assistance might be.

## Petitioning for help

Once inside the audience chamber, visitors are expected to speak their piece quickly. Slink is a busy man and has no time to listen to long-winded tales of woe. Most likely, he has heard them before. Any tendency to wander off topic or to explain the background to a petition will result in an impatient interruption urging the petitioner to get to the point. Fatty, the spokesman for the group, has a high raspy voice that becomes higher as he becomes more annoyed.

Most simple pleas for help will be granted if within the power of Slink and his gang. If the petitioner is a local resident of limited means, the request will most likely be fulfilled without any particular price attached, beyond a reminder that the petitioner owes Slink a favor.

Those of greater means, merchants and the like, or those requesting slightly larger favors, will find themselves surrendering a percentage of their business, or at the very least a hefty sum of money.

Anyone seeking a particularly difficult or dangerous service – breaking a pal out of the city gaol for example – will be required to agree to perform a major service for Slink.

Slink and his partners are prepared to consider almost any request. They are strictly businessmen in this matter, and have no moral qualms about contracting to kill, kidnap, or steal, though obviously the cost to the petitioner is considerably higher for these more nefarious deeds.

The following is a list of examples of small, large, and “special” favors, as well as some suggested services to be performed for special favors.

## Small favors

Help to recover a debt Help pay taxes

Help to get a job

Help get medical attention

## Large favors

Have someone killed

Have something valuable stolen

Defy or oppose the will of a nobleman or rich merchant Retrieve loved one (either kidnapped or willingly departed)

Discredit rival

Frame someone for a crime

Seek protection from a rival gang

## Special Favors

Defy the sovereign

Rescue someone from royal dungeon Kill a powerful figure

Oppose a Temple or other religious institution Oppose a wizard

## Quests for Slink

Retrieve a particular magical item from the city vaults. This item was stolen by Slink’s gang, but recovered by the authorities during the arrest of several of the perpetrators.

Embarrass and humiliate a rival gang leader by kidnapping a relative.

Kill a particularly troublesome law enforcement figure but make it look like an accident.

Escort someone to a distant city and then bring them back safely.

Replace a valuable object with a valueless duplicate and return with the original.

## Wizard’s Tower

**Role in the Campaign**

What wilderness would be complete without a lonely stone tower housing a mad wizard and his arcane experiments? What city lacks an imposing tower belonging to a powerful spell-caster, famous for her knowledge of local denizens and phenomena? Since RPGs have been around, it has been natural to associate towers and wizards. These places offer up boundless opportunities for adventure, housing either a patron, a mentor, an advisor, or a foe.

Usually built to ensure security and privacy, the average wizard’s tower (if one could be so bold as to speak of an average wizard) is built of stone and raised via magical means. It is designed to deter unwanted visitors, to prevent prying eyes, and to provide a quiet and structurally sturdy locale for experiments into new and dangerous magics.

Many wizards will build their towers in remote locations, and even those who choose to live in built-up areas will often locate their towers on the outskirts of settlements. As mentioned, wizards tend to value their privacy and security. Some civic minded mages also keep in mind the potential devastation that might be caused by an experiment gone wrong, and therefore keep their abode at a distance from innocent victims.

Wizardly towers tend to be dangerous places to attempt to penetrate. Aside for the

natural danger resident in the homeowner, these locales tend to be protected by a variety of guards and wards of an arcane nature. Rooftop gargoyles may animate, fearsome constructs may patrol the halls, and the building itself may turn against intruders, with shifting walls and magically locked doors. Some towers lack even a conventional entrance, with ingress and egress depending upon the magical ability to translocate.

Depending upon the nature of the campaign, wizards may play a central role in local government. In a magocracy, the local Lord will doubtless be a wizard, ruling the immediate area from his tower. In more mundane forms of government, some wizards may still be recognized as temporal Lords, depending upon their history of service to the crown. If not the local authority themselves, wizards often serve as advisors and defenders of

Depending upon the nature of the campaign, wizards may play a central role in local government. In a magocracy, the local Lord will doubtless be a wizard, ruling the immediate area from his tower. In more mundane forms of government, some wizards may still be recognized as temporal Lords, depending upon their history of service to the crown. If not the local authority themselves, wizards often serve as advisors and defenders of the local nobility. This depends very much on the personality of the wizards, as some prefer to be left alone to pursue their own agendas.

## Variations on the Theme

A particularly paranoid wizard might build a tower that is completely without windows or doors. The entire building might appear to be one seamless piece of stone or metal, defying anyone to gain entrance. Discussions with the locals will reveal that no one has ever seen anyone enter or leave the tower, but the wizard does simply show up in town sometimes… Is there a regular entrance hidden by illusions? Does the tower require some sort of magical entrance key? Or is there simply a hidden entrance somewhere (of somewhen?) else?

Powerful wizards may make their homes in various locations. One possibility for a wizards tower would be a multidimensional building in which each room actually existed in a different dimension; with doorways simply functioning as portals between them. Entering or leaving such a tower could have unexpected consequences as visitors find themselves deposited in unlikely places.

Similarly, a wizard’s tower might well move through time and space. Perhaps the entire tower moves via teleportation either at its master’s whim or in relation to an

external phenomena, like the phase of the moon. If an adventuring party is in need of assistance from a Wizard who lives in such a tower, their first quest might well be to figure out the pattern of movement of the tower and arrange to be in an appropriate location when it arrives!

## Sample: The Peculiar Tower of Petronious Prime

((Text to be read to players))

When bards gather to sing of the great mages, or when epic tales are told of wizards bold and grim, the name of Petronious Prime never comes up. However, should the conversation ever turn to wizards with particularly bizarre personalities, Petronious proud takes his place of honor.

Before you now, rising some five stories tall, lies the peculiar tower of Petronious Prime, an edifice of such renown that even the hardened adventurers among you cannot completely suppress a jolt of excitement. You are about to meet a legend.

((end player text))

## Overview

Petronious Prime is a wizard of some minor fame, less for his impressive contributions to the study of the arcance than for the bizarre magical tower in which he lives. The tower appears normal, if gaudy, from the exterior, but is anything but once visitors arrive within.

Petronious is now approaching 120 years of age and has spent almost his entire life studying what he describes as the magic of chaos. He has striven to harness unpredictable forces and make them predictable. He has delved deep into the mysteries of translocation magic and the links between the location of objects and their inherent magical energy. His life’s work is embodied in his tower, which to him serves as an almost living extension of his will, but for others serves as a sort of randomized series of unconnected places and things.

Famously irritable, Petronious receives few visitors. Gaining entry to his abode will not prove simple, but it can be achieved if visitors can convince his gatekeeper, a minor construct that Petronious joking calls a “chaos golem”, that their petition will be of interest to the mage.

What is the secret to Petronious’ famous tower? The contents of the tower randomly rearrange themselves at unpredictable intervals. This includes any visitors and their

possessions. Petronious appears able to exercise some control over this phenomena, but no one is sure how the transformations occur or how Petronious molds them to his will.

A typical transformation happens without warning. One moment a visitor might be sitting in a comfortable armchair on the third floor, and the next find himself seated on a potato sack in the cellar. The transformations seem to happen without rhyme or reason – room contents do not move together and sometimes pieces of furniture disappear completely, only to reappear at some random time in the future, likely in another room. During these transformations, the rooms themselves seem to move within the tower. One might be in a library, gazing out a ground floor window at one moment, and suddenly find oneself in a kitchen on the fifth floor.

Only Petronious is capable of interacting with the tower in a structured way. Somehow, the tower exercises a sort of prescience, knowing what Petronious wants or needs and ensures that the appropriate items are handy regardless of the apparent contents of the room. Thus, should Pertronious suddenly desire an apple from the pantry, he might open a drawer on a nearby desk, rummage briefly through the assorted papers and implements within, and then withdraw a fresh, juicy apple, still cool from the cellar.

This mysterious link between Petronious and the tower surprises and awes most visitors. When Petronious is ready to sleep, visitors suddenly realize that his bed is in the room. Should he desire to leave the tower, the front door is in the wall in front of him, even if moments before he was on the top floor. The tower reacts to Petronious’ whims as if it were sentient, but it is important to note that at no time will Petronious ever speak of the tower in this way, or act as if it were anything other than a normal house. He does not speak to the building (“Tower, get me some beer”), he merely wants a beer and the tower makes one available.

For anyone other than Petronious, the experience of his tower usually starts out as amusing, but often turns frustrating or even terrifying. Tales tell of one particular visitor, a distant and unpopular relative of Petronious, who spent a month seeking an exit. The wise man always looks to see what is on the opposite side of any door in the tower before passing through.

## Tower Exterior

The tower of Petronious Prime looks to be five stories tall, with an open roof-top garden. The walls are made of slick black stone of a variety unknown to any geologists in the party. The walls appear seamless, giving the appearance that the entire building

is carved from solid rock, and the only clue to its bizarre nature is the way in which windows randomly appear and disappear around the tower.

The front door is a sturdy, magical portal made of the same material as the rest of the tower exterior. It can only be opened from the inside or by the will of Petronious. It swings soundlessly on unseen hinges.

The keeper of the door is Willis, a chaos golem. Chaos golems are magical constructs, and Willis serves to keep the gawking masses from pestering Petronious. Willis appears on the wall of the tower, next to the front door, as a vaguely humanoid face which extrudes from the rock. Willis’ features are different every time he appears but sometimes resemble a caricature of a previous visitor. Willis will speak with visitors and should he be convinced of their bona fides, he will allow the door to open to permit them entry. It is important to note that Willis is no more predictable than anything else in the tower, and exactly what kinds of petitions he will find worthy varies greatly. Some days he may turn away an adventuring party on a noble quest while admitting someone with a less urgent need.

The position of the front door never appears to change – at least from the outside. It is always on the southern wall of the tower and it is surrounded by pleasant flower beds.

## Tower Interior

How does one describe the interior of rooms which may well change at any moment? Visitors to Petronious’ tower can count on few immutable facts, but there are a couple of things which appear to remain constant. Rooms within the tower are all round, approximately sixty feet in diameter, with a spiral stone staircase rising through the center of the room. Most rooms will have 1-3 windows, though the shape, location, and form of those windows may vary.

Doors, windows, and the stairs are perilous things within the tower, and wise visitors learn to stay in one place, rather than wandering through the structure. Anytime someone passes from one room to another or through a doorway, the DM should randomly determine what is on the other side. Even though it is possible to look through a doorway and see the room on the other side, there is no guarantee that what is seen will still be there when the doorway is traversed.

For the purposes of DMs running the tower, there is a flat 10% chance per 15 mins that the contents of the rooms will randomly change. There is also an 80% chance that

the contents of the rooms will change anytime someone goes up or down a flight of stairs, or uses a door.

The transformation takes place instantly and without warning. Interestingly, the phenomenon never results in injury to people in the tower. Furniture never materializes inside living organisms or in mid-air (where it might fall on unsuspecting victims). The contents of the ever changing rooms appear in a neatly arranged fashion, not a random jumble of furnishings.

Rooms appear to change in a completely random fashion, but generally follow relatively logical patterns (i.e. the contents of any given room will be more or less arranged by function so that beds don’t appear at the same time as the contents of the root cellar). There also appears to be an almost infinite combination of furnishings which come and go. It is dangerous to put anything possessions down in this tower, as the table they are placed on could well vanish (with the items on it) and not return to the tower for a very long time. The exception to this is of course the fact mentioned above that Petronious can retrieve any item he desires at will due to his bond with the tower.

It is important to note that due to the random nature of the shifting of the rooms, it is quite possible to become trapped in the tower unless Petronious himself leads you to the front door. Because the contents of the rooms shift whenever the stairs are used, and because the shifts are random, there is no guarantee that going downstairs to the “ground floor” will result in finding the exit. The main door to the tower is just as likely to be found on the third floor as the first, though through another quirk in the magic of the tower exiting the main door always deposits the traveler outside on the ground and never three stories in the air. Thus it is impossible to predict where one might find the way out, or the kitchen, the bedchambers. Indeed, without the active assistance of Petronious, the tower can become something of a house of horrors. Tales tell of some visitors who eventually threw themselves out of windows to escape.

As mentioned, when the rooms transform they tend to do so in such a way that their contents are distributed logically. When a DM determines that the rooms have shifted, he should consult the following tables to determine the general function of the new room and the new location of the room within the tower.

((begin tables)) Room Functions

Column 1 Column Two

01 – 05 Entry hall (includes main door!) 06 – 10 Kitchen

11 – 15 Bedroom

16 – 20 Storage Area

21 – 25 Roof-top Garden

26 – 30 Magical Laboratory

31 – 35 Library

((begin table)) Room Location
