((end table))

## Some sample rooms

**Entryway**

1. Doorway

The door to Petronious’ tower is surprisingly flimsy. It is neither warded by magic nor by a strong lock. The magical nature of the tower itself (not to mention his own power) is sufficient protection for Petronious. Anyone entering without the prior approval of Willis, the door warden, will find that as soon as they have passed through the doorway, the room will shift and become a dark, dank, dungeon cell without exits, and it will not shift again until Petronious wills it. Petronious usually remembers to check on prisoners regularly, but he is getting old…

1. Trapdoor

In theory, this trapdoor leads down into a root cellar and general storage area. Obviously, given the nature of the tower, it is just as likely to lead to the roof, or the bathroom.

1. Sitting area

A small sitting area appears recently vacated. A warm cup of mulled cider still sits beside an open journal and a dagger, the tip still wet with blood. A quick scan of the journal reveals, however, that the last entry was made over fifty years ago, apparently by a young woman. A careful read of the entire journal will show that it contains nothing of interest beyond the daily musings of a self-conscious young girl. Anyone familiar with the working of the tower will quickly surmise that these items have been randomly thrown together, but newcomers may find the table’s contents quite a puzzle.

1. Storage

Petronious uses the space behind the stairs in the entryway as a temporary storage area. Boxes, bags, chests, and assorted containers are piled haphazardly here. Although the exact configuration of the pile, as well as the contents of the containers, changes with every shift of the room, the area always remains a cluttered storage space.

1. Cupboards

A couple of tall cupboards are here, one containing hooks upon which are hung various coats, cloaks, hoods, and other outdoor wear. The other contains shelves filled with boots, walking sticks, gloves and similar accoutrements.

1. Stairway

A stone, spiral staircase leads upwards from the entryway to the next level of the tower. All staircases in the tower are identical, and with the exception of the entry level

and the roof-top garden, they all lead both up and down. The stairs are smooth, worn stone and are very treacherous if wet. Even when dry, any attempt to run up or down the stairs carries with it a significant chance of slipping and falling.

## Kitchen

1. Firepit

All cooking is done here, over a bed of hot coals. The fireplace has two rotisseries for roasting meat, as well as a small cauldron suspended over one side of the fire. Although no chimney is visible from the outside of the tower, from the inside the fireplace appears to connect to a flue. Where the smoke goes is something of a mystery.

1. Two long tables serve as preparation space. They are generally cluttered with food in various stages of preparation, kitchen implements, spices, and other cooking essentials. Although the tables always contain at least one (and usually several) completed dishes, suitable for a simple meal, at no time does Petronious actually cook. The food seems to appear from out of nowhere, every time the room shifts. It is always warm, tasty, and fresh.

1. Well

Despite the fact that the kitchen moves between floors like all the other rooms, it contains a small well. Although there is no logical explanation for its presence, the well always produces cold, fresh water. Anyone diving into the well will find that it is approximately 30 feet deep, and the bottom is littered with coins in various denominations, adding up to approximately $100.

1. Kitchen cupboards

These are standard cupboards containing the necessary utensils and flatware of a kitchen. The contents vary depending upon the shifting of the room with the simple pottery being replaced with fine china or silver place settings. Currently, the dishes in the cupboards are of very fine quality, with a set of crystal goblets bearing the crest of a long extinct noble family.

1. Storage

It appears that the kitchen has recently been resupplies, as bags of flour and boxes of various foodstuffs have been piled here pending unpacking. Several small kegs of fine brandy are included in the jumble.

## Magical Laboratory

This floor is dedicated to Petronious’ ongoing magical research. As might be expected, much of his work relates to multi-dimensional spaces and teleportation. The lab should contain at least 3 minor and 1 major magic items in a functional condition and a similar number of non-functional prototypes.

Summoning circle

At one time, Petronious’ research focused on the summoning of extra-dimensional beings. This graven circle of protection was a part of those experiments and served him well in numerous summoning rites. Although he no longer dabbles in such matters, the circle remains functional and requires only the appropriate rituals and invocations to be reactivated.

1. Bookcases

Petronious has traveled widely throughout multiverse, and collected obscure tomes and texts related to things magical. At any given time, there are between 50 and 70 books of lore on these shelves. Most are written in obscure, dead languages, often from other dimensions or planes. Careful study, usually with magical assistance to decipher the arcane languages will allow a wizard character to learn one previously unknown spell per month of study.

1. Work Area

These tables are littered with papers, components, tools, and other paraphernalia related to the creation of magical items. Although Petronious rarely crafts items these days, the area is littered with the detritus of his previous work.

A careful search of the work area will turn up 4-40 components of use in spell casting. 10% of these will be rare, exotic, or expensive components such as the congealed blood of a near extinct monster. 30% will be relatively uncommon, such as powdered gemstone, while 60% will be common components.

The tables will also contain at least 1 functional magic wand, 3 scrolls containing arcane spells, and one miscellaneous magical item. The DM should decide the exact nature of these magic items, depending of course upon the magic level of the campaign in question, but the miscellaneous magic item at least should be an exotic object, never before encountered by the adventuring party. It might well be a beneficial but perilous item, or possible a long lost treasure of renown. Keep in mind though that Petronious is not likely to part easily with any of these items.

1. Strange Portal

A 5-foot diameter sphere of blue light, crackling with energy, hovers about a foot off of the ground. Even the most mundane of characters will be able to sense without any effort that the sphere exudes intense magical energy.

Those more attuned to the arcane will be able to sense the chaotic, turbulent flows of magic that surround the sphere. The portal itself is the heart of the tower, responsible for the extra-dimensional transport of the towers contents. A careful study of the portal – something which would require at least ten years of research – would eventually reveal that the portal exists simultaneously in over a hundred different dimensions.

The portal is, however, an extremely dangerous artifact to experiment with, as it is completely uncontrollable. Merely being in the presence of the portal causes items to randomly transport themselves between dimensions, as evidenced by the behaviour of items within the tower. Anything or anyone that actually enters the portal will be instantly transported at random to another dimension. Unfortunately, each item’s destination is determined at random, so a fully equipped adventurer entering the portal will almost certainly arrive naked. Even if one were fortunate enough to travel to the same dimension as one’s equipment though, one will discover that the actual spatial location of arrival in the new dimension is also completely random, so the traveler and his gear could still arrive in widely separated locations. It is also important to note that there is no “other end” of the portal at the destination, making returning to Petronious’ tower virtually impossible.

Needless to say, adventurers are strongly advised against entering the portal. Anyone casting detection magics on the portal will get a sense of strong chaotic magic and a sense of strong foreboding. DMs should advise players that their characters feel very uneasy about the portal, and even the most mundane of characters should sense something “wrong” about the sphere. Given these hints, anyone choosing to enter the sphere deserves their fate!

If asked, Petronious will be unable to explain the origin of the portal. If pressed about his apparently unique relationship with the chaos of the portal, such as its apparent ability to anticipate his desires and reshape items at his whim, he will speculate that the portal itself has a limited sentience. The study of the Chaos Portal in fact makes up the bulk of his life’s work.

# ESSENTIAL PLACES BIBLE MATERIAL

What is needed is a book full of locations with floor plans in varied scale as appropriate to the size of the place/levels or floors depicted, each plan showing fixtures, furniture, doors and windows, etc. With that depiction there needs be s selection of persons, several each proprietor and worker types and their general abilities, and guidelines for the numbers and sorts of other persons likely encountered in the establishment. With that information for commercial places there needs be a list of goods purveyed and/or services offered. Concluding the treatment for a location, a few brief adventure hooks.

About the only prefatory text really needed is an introductory bit, a discussion of how to make the standard floor plans varies so as to make the same sort of place seem different in a different location, and a discussion of the monetary system employed in the work, how to adapt it to a specific game system--for the one to be used will fit in any literary work simply by using specific coin names.

On request Gary Gygax will supply electronic files from his mss. for the information in the works cited hereafter, and more details of the LA game monetary system if needed.

## Special thanks:

John Morrow for his initial work on this project.

Gary Gygax for the monetary system locations, and extensive lists of prices for

## EQUIPMENT LISTS

**Note on Prices and Selling**

The prices listed for goods and materials represent average values. Depending on the location, rarity, or disposition of the seller, actual costs may vary—sometimes rising to double the listed amount or falling to half or less. Such adjustments are entirely at the discretion of the Game Master, reflecting local economy, scarcity, or simple opportunism.

When selling items to a merchant or purveyor, characters can expect to receive anywhere from 20% to 70% of that merchant’s usual selling price. Thus, an item worth 100 in local currency might bring as little as 20 or as much as 70 when sold, depending on market conditions and the negotiator’s skill. As always, supply and demand in the setting dictate the outcome.

Should a character establish their own place of business, prices are naturally theirs to set. Sales and trade in this case should be resolved through the Game Master’s impartial adjudication, balancing opportunity, realism, and narrative flow.

**Antiquities**

The sale of antiquities follows different principles, as such items often carry historical or cultural value beyond their material worth. Antiquities generally fall into three broad classes:

**Coins**: In smaller or isolated communities, old coins are usually treated as little more than curiosities—worth about 10% to 20% less than, or at best equal to, current currency. In more sophisticated or urban markets, however, collectors and numismatists may pay a premium. As a rough measure, small quantities of ancient coins might fetch between 200% and 600% (roll one six-sided die) of their metal value. Larger hoards, being less rare, usually earn a lesser increase—typically 120% to 220% (roll two six-sided dice).

**Jewelry**: Apply the same general rules as for coins, though quantity is rarely a factor. Determine the value of any gemstones included and add their full worth to the metal value before applying the multiplier for antiquity. The craftsmanship, age, and provenance of a piece may significantly enhance its price.

**Objects d’Art**: This category includes all manner of valuable artifacts—antique armor, garments, paintings, tapestries, sculpture, and other works of artistry or devotion. The worth of such items depends entirely on the world of your campaign. Use real-world analogues as a guide, adjusting for rarity and the degree of interest in such things among the people and cultures of your setting.

Arbitrary values are acceptable, provided they remain reasonable and do not eclipse the broader balance of the campaign—except in cases where a singular treasure serves as the central reward of a great adventure. Truly priceless items may exist, but as in all tales, _the really big one usually gets away._

A treasure valued at a million in local currency is not implausible, especially if divided among a party of adventurers—but even then, fortune is fickle. Players may sell too cheaply, misjudge a relic’s worth, or fall prey to swindlers and con artists. Such moments often lead to as much storytelling delight as the adventure itself.

Markets may shift, demand may vanish, or fakes may circulate. The Game Master should use these circumstances to enrich the world and remind players that, in trade as in treasure-hunting, luck and cleverness are as valuable as gold.

**_Extraordinary Items:_** Sale of Extraordinary things is also a different matter.

_Memory Tablets:_ These are pretty dear, and something in the neighborhood of a

$10,000 value is not outrageous. In short supply, the cost might be more than twice that.

of course! Guild members will possibly receive a discount.

_Powers and Like Extraordinary Ability Related Knowledge:_ If for example, your campaign assumes that any Power or the like is worth about at least $1,000 per Grade or AEP it costs to use it, then the sale of such thing to a guild will fetch about half price, to an individual whatever that one will pay. Unique ones, though, are worth at least double the price of the “known and standard” ones.

_Extraordinary Items:_ These are not considered as things one is able to purchase. Their sale value to other than an Avatar is basically the sum of the price of a non- Extraordinary item of the same sort, plus from $1,000 to $10,000 for each special feature. Consider protection bonuses and striking plus harm ones at about the middle to top range and multiply per point. A long spear that adds 10 to weapon precision and 6-8 points of harm is thus worth its normal weapon base plus $72,000 to $90,000 more for its addition to success in striking and its harm. A sword surely has the $10,000 per point addition. Use common sense and base it on the way you want your campaign to run. Any supernatural bonus, 25 or more precision bonus, 17 or more harm bonus, _doubles_ the cost for such. If the long spear noted above was +25 weapon precision and added 17-20 harm. The price would be normal weapon base plus $150,000 to $200,000 doubled,

i.e. $300,000 to $400,000.

Repair: Typical items: are at $1 per harm point to be restored or else 40% to 60% original cost of item.

c. 70% for complex items, and repair cost is paid before success is determined.

Poor tinkers will add 2 points, average ones will add 4, expert ones will add 6 points as noted.

## Add the following to the list contained in the core book:

Oracalcum: $5,000/ounce. This rare metal is called “golden copper” although it is neither gold nor copper. It is a non-tarnishing, non-corroding, non- conductive metal. It has a weight of about double that of aluminum (half that of steel), is flexible yet resilient when forged to be so, unyielding as an iron bar likewise. As it is exceptionally difficult to work, oracalcum objects of any sort cost twice the base price for a normal object, plus the weight of the oracalcum metal. A coin of this metal the size of a silver ounce weighs approximately one-half ounce. Were it accepted as legal tender, its value will be ten times that of gold, $5,000 per ounce, perhaps

more because of its rarity.

PROTECTION AND COMMAND CIRCLE MATERIALS FOR GEOURGY, NECROURGY & SORCERY

It is generally required that a geourge, necromancer, or sorcerer possess both protection and command circle materials in order to perform acts of summoning, calling, commanding, or creating otherworldly forces or entities. These materials are typically first acquired as part of a practitioner’s magical training or initiation, often drawn from specialized guilds, colleges, or private arcane sources. Such items are costly and difficult to obtain, ranging in value from several thousand to tens of thousands in local currency, depending on availability and quality.

The question naturally arises as to how long these materials last before they must be replenished, for even the finest substances wear thin with repeated use.

If one adopts a lenient interpretation, the materials might suffice for as many as twelve complete pairs of circles before exhaustion sets in and a fresh supply must be acquired. The moderate stance allows seven sets of circles to deplete the stock. The strictest view holds that three sets are all that may be formed before the materials are entirely consumed—three times being the charm, as the saying goes.

In all cases, it should be remembered that such organizations and institutions providing these materials rely on more than membership dues to keep their doors open. The expense of resupply may well serve as a reminder that power has its price, and that the path of the summoner is never maintained without cost.

In many fantasy settings, priests, theurges, and other divine practitioners require both propitiation and votive summoning materials to perform sacred rites or channel divine power. These materials represent the tangible means by which a servant of the gods communes with higher powers, honors divine will, or invokes a response through ritual. The precise nature of such items depends heavily on the deity, faith, and cosmology of the campaign world. Each pantheon and tradition defines what is holy, acceptable, or forbidden; thus, the specifics of these materials must be shaped by the Castle Keeper or Game Master to suit the setting’s theology and tone.

Still, some general principles can be applied across systems and faiths to guide their use in play:

Propitiation Materials are offerings made to appease, honor, or seek favor from a deity or spiritual force. They may include incense, sacred oils, blessed candles, libations, or symbolic items of sacrifice. The value and rarity of such materials should scale with the importance of the rite being performed—minor blessings requiring only common materials, while greater invocations or miracles demand rare, costly, or personally significant offerings.

Votive Summoning Materials serve as conduits for summoning or manifesting divine energy—whether to call forth celestial servitors, sanctify a place, or perform acts of theurgy. These may take the form of relics, inscribed seals, blessed symbols, or carefully prepared components aligned with the deity’s domain. The exact items should reflect both the character’s faith and the setting’s cosmology.

When determining the use or cost of these materials, consider three elements: the rite’s magnitude, the sacred context, and the moral or spiritual weight of the act. A simple prayer for healing might consume a pinch of consecrated dust, while a grand invocation to repel a demon lord could require a gemstone etched with runes and anointed in sanctified oil beneath a full moon.

These guidelines are intentionally flexible to suit any system or campaign. The key principle remains constant across worlds: the physical act of devotion—through offering, sacrifice, or ritual focus—links the mortal to the divine, binding faith to tangible expression within the game world.

**Propitiation Materials:** Blessed oil Blessed water

## Craftsmanship & Rarity Multiplier to Artistic Item Value,

**Including Fine Furniture, Jewelry, Porcelain, Pottery, and Table Service Ware:**

The workmanship of most items is a major factor in their value. So too the rarity of the item. Craftsmanship and rarity are especially important in areas where considerable expertise and artistry can be demonstrated. Generally, the quality of workmanship will add immensely to base worth of various items, as will the number of like items extant, and to reflect this in generic fashion, the table of multipliers below will serve the Game Master well:

Fine Furniture, Jewelry, Porcelain, Pottery, and Table Service Ware

_Multiplier_

.10 to .50

1

_Multiplier_

Common and quantities available:

.10 to .50

1

Uncommon

2

Rare

5

10

One of a few

30

Nonesuch

60

Apply multipliers by adding them together. Do not apply one and then the other, as that inflates the worth far too much.

Example: A badly damaged signed masterwork that is the only example of its kind known (.20 x 40 + 60 [100] = 20 times normal value for a similar object, even though this item is badly damaged.
